Moore Sewerage Scheme.

The fortnightly meetingl of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton. on Tuesday, when- there ware present :—Nlessrs. S. Woodward (nreaiding), C. Roylance, A. Greenway. J. The Examiner, Saturday, December 16, 1905.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES. I NEW LIBERAL MINISTRY.

Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman has lost no time in forming and announcing to the public; an exception- ally strong and well balanced Cabinet. The Prime ...- . . .

Minister had an audience with the Kingson.Sunday

evening and submitted his list of proposed Ministers. The reason for this somewhat unusual course is worth

a word of explanation. Mr. Balfour resigned on Monday last week, and since than there has been no Government, a serious matter in an Empire of world- wide power. It was founds on Saturday, however, that Sir Henry was ready with the list of proposed Ministers, and, that fact being communicated to the King, his Majesty, after attending Divine service at Wimborne on Sunday, travelled back to town. Between six and seven in the evening. the. Prime Minister went to Buckingham Palace, had a 'private audience of the King lasting about three-quarters of an hour, and obtained his Majesty's approval of the names set out above. _ _ _

Monday was a great day for politicial leaders. Members of the late Government attended a Privy

Council, held at noon at Buckingham Palace, and delivered up their seals of office to the King. At a later hour the members of the new Liberal Govern- ment met at another Privy Council and leceived their

respective seals from his *Majesty. Parliament was formally prorocmed to January 15th, and it was unofficially announced that one additional appointment in the new Ministry had been made—Mr. George Whiteley, member for the Pudsey Division of Yorks'ire, becoming Patronage Secretary to the Treasury, or Chief Liberal Whip. Shortly before half-past three the new Ministers began to arrive at Buckingham Palace. The fug had lifted, the sun shone brightly, and all the appearances indicated an auspicious start for the Government. Many people had gathered at the gates to watch the arrivals, and some of the earlier among them were recognised and respectfully saluted as they passed in. Mr. John Barns went to the Palace wearing a reefer

===== 0002601_19051216_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON BUTCHER HEAVILY FINED.

At Warrington, on Monday, John Preece, butcher, 3. Winwick-street, and Pierpoint-street, was charged with having deposited for sale meat which was diseased and unfit for human consumption.—Mr. Hallaway, depnty Town Clerk, apneared to prosecute, and stated that on November 17th Inspector Flood passed the defendant's shop in Pierpoint-street. and on looking through the window saw some beef which did not appear to be satisfactory. He proceededto the Winwick-street shop, and having obtained the key to the Pierpoint- street premises returned and examined the beef, which he found was diseased.--inspector Flood, in answer to Mr. Steel, who represented defendant, said anyone who had dealt in beef would know at II glance that the meat was diseased.—Dr. Gornall said he had examined the beef, and found that it was that of an animal in an advanced stage of tuberculosis.—Mr. Steel for the defendant ',admitted that the meat was diseased. Last week, he Said, a Mr. Kershaw, of Glaze- bury, called on Preece and persuaded him to purchase the Ca•CAIIR of a cow which he had killed. The meat was broneht to Warrington by a butcher named Evans who could see that it was not all right. Evans took it to the Pierpoint-street shop and then went to tell Mr. Preece about the condition of the meat. While he was absent I spector Flood called. Had defendant seen the meat it would never have been offered for sale.— The magistrates considered the case fully proved and inflicted a penalty of £lO and costs. WARRINGTON MEN'S EXTRAORDINARY ESCAPADE. The story of an extraordinary escapade was related to the Warrington Bench on Monday when James Broad- hurst, 84, Mersey-street, Alban Stirrup, 46, Pinner's Brow, and John O'Neill, 13, Cockhedge-lane, were charged with stealing a horse and cab, of the value of £7O, the property (if Mr. Edward Connor, Sanl-ey-street, and further with cruelly ill-treating a horse.—Mr.Steel appeared for Stirrur —The evidence showed that at about eleven o'clock on Saturday. night a cab was sent for from Mr. Connor's, and was at once despatched to the Royal Exchang3, Golden-square. On arrival, the cabman went inside to look for his fgre, and when he came out he found the horse and cab missing. He was told that some men—two on the box, and one inside had driven off with it. The prisoners were all concerned in the matter, as after events clearly proved. It was stated that they drove at a furious pace down Winwick- street and Orford-lane, and then down by Winwick-road. and eventually they were met in Marsh House-lane by P.C. Kenwright. On seeing him, two of the men jumped from the cab, and ran away. Kenwright followed, and after a most excit- ing chase he succeded in catching O'Neill. With the assistance of P.C. Carbett he brought the horse and cab to the Police Station, and they were afterwards taken to Mr. Connor's. The h..lrse was in a pitiable condition, white with lather, and an expert witness stated that it must have been terribly over-driven.— The charge of theft was withdrawn, as the Chief Con- stable said it would be difficult to prove felonious intent, and on the second charge, the prisoners were each fined 10s. and costs.

A YOUTH CHARGED WITH STEALING A BICYCLE.-

PECULIAR CASE.

At the Dareshury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, a youth named F. W. Stirton. of Grappenhall, IVEIR summoned for stealing a bicycle on August 24, of the value of £5, the property of James Burgess, Antrobua. Mr. Browne appeared for the defendant.—Complainant stated that on the date named he went to Stretton Walking Day. and left his bicycle alongside a fence. He left it at eight o'clock, but at nine o'clock it was miss- ing. There was an old bicycle there, and he took it informed the police.On November 12 he went to defend- ant's house, and defendant told him that he was at Stratton when a man asked him to exchange bicycles end pay for a drink. He exchanged,and gave him three- r mce. He (the defendant) gave 10s, for his bicycle.— Mr. Browne stated that the defendant had said quite consistently that he had not stolen the machine. The man with whom the bicycle was exchanged said that he was hard up and wanted a drink. Seeing that the bicycle was a better one than his own, the defendant consented to exchange. Since August 24 to November 12 the defendant had been riding the bicycle in the' very neighbourhood where it was supposed to have been stolen.—Mr. Long said the defendant had got into a very awkward position, hut as there had not been any felonious intent the Bench would dismiss the case.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist. ']

It is very gratifying to find the Warrington men showing such improved form, and by the time all the injured men are on active service again I think the Club should be capable of holding its head as high as ever. Neither Fish, Widdeson, nor Harris were avad- able on Saturday, but the locals gave a smart display against Runcorn, and won by two tries to nil. Both forward and btck there was a noticeable improvement in pace and combination, and though the Linnets played tvell they were clearly the inferbr team.

It is no secret that the Runcorn men, who have a much better record than Warrington so far, fancied their chances at Wilderspool. They made a deter- mined fight too, but were scarcely clever enough to penetrate the home defence. In the first half they had a good share of the play, but after Kenyon scored the opening try for Warrington the visitors scarcely ever looked like troubling the scorers. Jolley was übiqui- tous at the base of the scrummage, but relied too much on tricky putting on, and did a great deal too much punting. The Warrington halves appeared to have learned something from the " All , Blacks "and got the ball away to the threequarters in smart style. Once Hockenhall drew the defence one way and then threw back to the other wing in skilful fashion. It was quite a New Zealand dodge and Isherwood, would have scored easily but bra knock-on. There is too much sameness as a rule in Warrington's attacking methods, and I hope the visit to Leeds on Wednesday convinced them that there is nothing to equal well-judged passing, coupled with brainy execu- tion. The " All Blacks " could win nearly every game they play without either kicking the ball or making a forward rush. Most teams would be anchored to one spot. It only shows what can be accomplished by clever combination.

Since the close of the war fifty-five constituencies in Great Britain have been contested, and the result com- pare-I with the last previous election, has beers as follows : Liberal by-election vote Liberal previous election vote 105,322

As compared with 1000 the Liberals havo gained 21 Iseats, nut in 1900 the khaki fever WIIS at its height, and the party fared badly. On the other hand. in 188.5 Liberals were at their hip.h water mark, and it will be seen that they have not only reco 7ered the position in seats which they held then, but, in regard to the fifty-live constituencies, now hold twelve more seats than in 1885. The net result, therefore, is not merely "better than in 185," but "half as well again as in 1885."

Hockey.

Stockton Heath travelled to New Brighton on Satur- day to play their match with the Wanderers, and as they wereat full strength for the first time for some weeks, it was hoped that they would be able to make a hard fight. On arriving at the ground and inspecting the playing pitch, one received rather a shock, for the ground was lumpy and so small as to make one think it is not up to the smallest standard size required by the Association. The Wanderers seemed to take the match very seriously, as they had their best possible side out, Including the four county men who did duty for Cheshire in the county match on the Warrington Cricket Field last week.

From the commencement Stockton Heath attacked. and after 10 minutes play Banks opened tbe score. Here- abouts a great misfortunes befell the visitors. J. G Hatton getting a nasty hit just below the knee, although he pluck ly kept on playing, he was unable to turn smartly, and this gave the Wanderers a weak spot to play upon. This they did not fail to do, and within a few minutes they drew level. This wa.s.the half-time score, for although the Stockton Heath forwards kept play within the Wanderers' half, they failed to make much head way against MClelland and his fellow defenders.

On changing over, for the first, quarter of an hour the Stockton Heath defence was all at sea, and when the Wanderers added three more goals, it looked as if the visitors would go home with almost a cricket score against them. But the game fluctuated and Stockton Heath added two more goals, thanks to smart play by Banks and Richardson. Just before time the Wanderers came again, and the outside left being allowed to go on from an absolutely offside position, a fifth goal was added from his centre. Result: Wanderers, 5; Stockton Heath, 3.

The Wanderers will be a hard "nut " far any side on their own ground, as being all big heavy fellows, the narrow ground gives them a better chance of tackling more nimble opponents, while their short sharp rushes are just the game to suit the short ground. I shall con- fidently look to Stockton Heath to avenge their defeat in the return, of course providing the present team keeps together.

The fez= misses C. H. Bond at outside left very much, his successor not having the amount of speed re- quired in an outside position. It will be a long time before an equal to " Kelowna Bill" will be found.

The second eleven were defeated 1-0 after a hard game, but a draw would have been abetter result,though one most not overlook the fact that. the visitors turned out with only 10 players.

It might be interesting to compare the records for last season with those of this ;- - -- Goals.

The first team record is really better this season than appears from above figures, because 23 of the 44 goals were attained against the Backfield club. With regard to the second team record, one harily knows how to approach the subject, for without a doubt they have a much finer set of forwards than was the case last season, yet they fail to get goals. Do they try to be too clever in the circle, if so they might drop pretty work, and let its have a little more deadly shooting. I feel sure S. Baxter will show them how to shoot. He is fine inside forward and Stockton Heath are fortunate in having such a capable man handy.

WIFE JUNIPS THROCGIi A WINDOIV.

At the DAreshury Petty Sessions on Tuesday Albert Moss Dobqon, Grice-street,Stockton Ifeatli,was charged with ill-treating his three children and also with per- sistant cruelty to his wife. Mr. Jenkins appeared for Mr. Dobson and Mr. Knowles condneted the prosecu- tion for family neglect, on behalf of. the N.S.P.C.C.— Mr. Browne defended.—The evidence showed that on many occasions the defendant had ill-used his wife. One night he was locking the bedroom door, and she was so afraid of him that she jumped through the window, breaking her leg, and was an inmate of the in- firrnary for several weeks. Whilst she was away prisoner ill-treated the children.—The bench granted at separation order, defendant to contribute 12s. a week towards the maintenance of his wife. A fine of ss. was imposed in each of two cases for using had language, and defendant was sentenced to three months with hard labour for ill-treating the children. I . .

Sir HENRY CAMPBELL- THE RIG EIT MEN BA.NNIMMAN has pro. IN THE greased nobly with his RIGHT PLACES. Ministerial appoint..

merits. The list of I members as it stands at the present moment must be quite surprising to those who supposed—some of wirmi quite honestly supposed—that there was no material on the Opposition benches that might 1n44 be sneered at after the manner, let us say, of Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN'a construction of his Ministry has been a revelation to the country. The Liberal party in the House of Commons at the present moment is a small party. That is one of the things that is, perhaps, unnecessary to say. It is on the smallness of the party that the usual

Conservative insult has been based. "Would you turn us out to let such men in P" has been the usual kind of taunt. We know, of course, where it comes from. "Do you think James, that anybody would murder me to make you King ?" was a question put by Charles 11. to his brother, the Duke of York. It is easier to imitate a jest than to make one. Indeed, the great joke about the Conservative party is that it has remained in power so long, in spite of the

detestation in which it was so clearly held by the constituencies. The whirligig of Time now brings about its revenues. From that remnant of the Liberal party, which has been jeered by the mean- est set of men that ever held office, there has been formed what is admittedly the most remarkable Ministry of modern times.

And what do we mean by " remarkable "is Simply this, that the Ministry formed by Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN contains more brains, more experience, more administrative ability, and more general capacity than any Ministry which has been formed within living memory. All previous Prime Ministers have been hampered in forming their Governments by what Lord RANDOLPH CHURCHILL called with his utmost brutal candour, " the old gang." In Lord RAmooLru's sense, this meant a lot of rather worn-out men in the House of Commons. It has not been necessary, in constructing a new Liberal Ministry, to clear out any "old gang." Time has done the work. Mr. GLADSTONE was much im- peded when forming a ministry by the claims of men who had been something at some pre- vious time, and who were resolute on being something again. As many of these were in the House of Lords, much of the Ministry had therefore to be placed in the Upper Chamber—an arrangement which was in every way disadvan- tageous to the Administrations formed by Mr. GLADSTONE. For members of the House of Lords, when they are included in a Liberal Ministry, are exceedingly liable to turn recalcitrant. The typical instance is the late Duke of Anonn. It happily occurs that Sir HENRY CAMPBELL- BANNERMAN has not been embarrassed by the claims of worn-out members of the Peerage. There is no member of his Ministry who could be spared; and to see how strong and capable is the Ministry. it is merely necessary to compare man for man the men who are going out with the men who are corning in. Further appointments are still being made. They are all of an excep- tionally satisfactory character. Sir HENRY CAMPBELL•BANNERMAN has assigned no post to any man who was not likely to fill it with dis- tinction on the ground that he has been a mem- ber of a previous Liberal Government. There has been no favouritism ; there has been no kow-tow- ing to claims of rank or precedence. There has simply been a solid determination to put the right men in the right places with the result that the Liberal Party will be able to present to the country a united front,and an irresistible claim for a national mandate.

The fight has begun A CRO•FIELD and the rallying A CROSFIELD ! cry of the Liberal

fowrces is oncii;-i-nOi; " A Crosfield—Crosfield for ever ! " Five years ago, Mr. A. H. Crosfield stepped into the breach and fought the battle of Liberalism for Warring- ton when the fortunes of the party were at the lowest ebb and when the tide of popular opinion was on the side of the Conservative Government. His self-sacrifice and pluck on that occasion earned him the enduring affection and esteem of all Warrington Liberals. What matters it that they were beaten and by a record majority! Not much certainly, for little else could be expected under the circumstances. The party lost the day, but gained a standard-bearer of inestimable worth. We know of no honester politician, nor of a more earnest and thorough-going economist and progressive than Mr. A. H. Crosfield. It is a matter for great congratulation that he has again consented to fight the Liberal battle,and there WAS no questioning the unanimity and heartiness of his adoption as the candidate on Wednesday. He was cheered to the echo, and we only hope that those present will translate their enthusiasm into actions between now and the declaration of the poll. They have an excellent candidate, and a unique causq. Has not Free Trade been assailed,the sacred tradition of freedom under the British flag been abused in the Trans- veal, and at home legislation enacted which has 'increased the taxes of the workingman and pro- vided doles for the friends of the Government? Surely these things, with the grand old party watchwords of the past, are enough to spur every Liberal and Labour man in Warrington to unite against the common enemy, the retrogressive and Protection-tainted Tory as typified by the sitting member, Mr. Robeh Pierpoint ! We have no fear as to the result if all pull together and keep the great nationhl issues to the forefront. That 1165 khaki majority will go the way of nearly all the illusory figures which have been challenged in the 56 by-elections since the war and the introduction , of so-called tariff reform.

Th e unemployed have already approached the new Government. 0 n Wednesday a deputation waited on the Prime Minister, but the most dramatic feature of the occasion was not the attitude of the Prime Minister, but the presence by his side of John Burns—facing the unemployed whom he used to address on Tower Hill. "At a moment" says the Daily News "when unemployment seems to be a problem beyond the resources of statesman- ship a man of the people arises to advise the governing class which has filed in its efforts. This is one of those facts which mark a permanent change in the spirit of a nation's history." It is very satisfactory to leArn that the attitude of the two Ministers was not only sympathetic but indicative of a real determination to act. The Premier said "there was great force in the con- tention as to the inadequacy of public charity, and added that the Government would approach the question with a desire to discover the sources of the evil and to prevent its recurrence." There can be no question that the personnel of the new ministry is such as to give both the workless and the workers the best promise of material aid and benefit for which they have y3t dared to hope.

HOPE FOR THE WORKLESS.

Probably th e ANOTHER SPECIAL COUNCIL Mayor regrets by MEETING, now, his ill-con-

sidered and uneon- stitutional closure of the Town Council meeting last week, for there is no ddubt that a lot of resentment both in the Chamber and among the general public was caused thereby. Moreover, he ' has been obliged to call a special meeting of the Council for this (Friday) evenieg in response to a requisition signed by nineteen members—a clear majority of the Council. This, in sonie respects amounts to e vote of censure, and it at least indi- cates that the Council will not tolerate an auto- cratic curtailment of its rights and privileges by the Mayor. It is impossible to avoid noticing that the list of those who signed the requisition for a special meeting contains the name of only one Alderman. One of the arguments against the office of Alderman is that its fine freedom from electoral control tends to make these "reverend seigneurs" conservative in tendeory and inclined to resent popular clamoar, as clamour per se, however just may be its cause. It would certainly seem that the Aldermen of Warrington are out of sympathy with the Councillors and not inclined to make a bother about the preservation of the privileges of the Council. This is to be regretted, as it lends colour to the theory that there is a somnolent and enervating atmosphere about the Alderrnanic " bench " which has a weakening effect on the aldermanic vertebrre.

resolution accepting the tender for compounding the hears engine at Winwick for £1530, and then to discuss the position of the Corporation in regard to the application to the Local Government Board for sanction to the borrowing of the money, and to pass resolutions thereon. As we announced in our issue of last Friday, the Local Government Board has been strongly memorialised against granting the loan, the appeal being signed by a majority of the members of the Council and by other prominent ratepayers. Substantially the argument is that the beam engine is more fitted for the scrap-heap than compounding, and is most certainly not worth the expenditure of £1,600. Presumably the Water Committee and its officers hold other views, but a good deal of weighty and expert opinion against the compounding could be submitted to the Council. The fact must not be forgotten, too,that the scheme was only sanctioned at the July Council meeting by the vote of the Mayor after there had been 12 for and 12 against. Since then the opposition to the scheme and to the expenditure involved has been steadily growing until now only a small minority are in favour of it. We hope that the matter will he fully re-con- sidered to-night. If it is, there is no doubt that the matured verdict will be against any expendi- ture on compounding the engine at all. Some more economical and feasible method of increasing the engine-power at WinwiCk will need to be devised, and the cost therefore charged to the revenue account of the Department. A direct levy from the rates for such a purpose is more than the harassed ratepayer*. can be expected t 5 endure with equanimity.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Crosfield for Warrington—this time—and no error!

- * January Bth is said to be the date decided upon for the dissolution. The writs will then immediately be issued and the elections proceeded with. * * * The Lancashire and Cheshire Miners' Federation, which has about 30,000 members, has granted a pension of 355. per week to Mr. Sam Woods, who is in delicate health.

* * First School Boy (reading a placard) : I say the King's sent for C.-B. Second School Boy (also in the eleven) : Good old Fry !—" Punch."

* , * Out of the £400,000,000 the late Government had spent above normal expenditure, old-age pensions could have been given to men over 40 years of age, never mind 60.

* It is curious that the only two politicians of the day in England, who are generally described as "honest" are members of the new Cabinet—" Honest" John Morley and" Honest "John Burns.—" Truth." * * * Heckling has already commenced. In our corres- pondence columns is a short letter from Mr. F. Monks in which he propounds questions for answering by both the Parliamentary candidates for the Borough, in reference to Home Rule and Tariff Reform.

As a result of the tree-planting movement, the open space in Knutsford-road looks like a miniature shubbery, and the harsh outlines of the square in front of Arpley Station have been relieved. On Thursday young plane, elm, and sycamore trees were plantedinside the rails on the Railway Co.'s property, and in tho coal-yard opposite. We feel sorry for the trees, for they will need to live the strenuous life all their days amid such surroundings. But they are being martyred in a good cause. * * It is reported that there is a feeling of doubt at Conservative headquarters in Warrington about the ability of Mr. Robert Pierpoint to hold the fort success- fully once again. Tories are alarmed at the bold showing of the Liberals, and the hostility of the Free Trade Unionists, but it is no good wishing for the leadership of Sir Gilbert, with his money-bags and beer-barrels. The " member " has opened his campaign, and we wish him a gallant fight and an honourable " death " on the political field. * 5 * It is just a year since the Progressive Labour League was formed, and it has fully justified its existence. Free Trade is sure of a cordial backing from the intelligent workers of Warrington, and the League is doing much to fOCUti the Labour forces that they may present a united opposition to the policy of reaction. The members had on Friday the pleasure of listening to an address by Mr. Crosfield, who urged the necessity of such vital reforms as the taxation of land values and a graduated income tax. The enthusiasm with which the resolution recommending Mr. Crosfield as candidate was passed, augurs well for the part which Labour will play in the great cor.test. * * * The Women's Liberal Association must have felt a thrill of pride in its official bosom on Tuesday evening when aboutisoo members and an equal number of friends attended the reception held in Parr Hall by Miss Cros- field and her brother the candidate. There was not much speechmaking, but plenty of " coffee and conversation," and possibly some conversion. All the wives and daughters who were present in such a large majority only need to spur on the men-folk on the day of the election for the Women's Liberal Association to exercise an unprecedented influence on the issue of the fight. We hope they will, this time, see their enthusiastic endeavours rewarded by success. * * The late Conservative agent for the Altrincham Division, Mr.F.R.B. Lindsell, addressed a meeting of the Free Trade League in Warrington on Thursday evening, and put some very effectual nails in the Protectionist coffin. "It was," lie said, "the duty of Conservatives to pat principle before party on a vital question like this." He contended that Protection would reduce work instead of giving more, and the price of goods to the consumer would increase, while he could not find from the Board of Trade returns that any of the industries Mr. Chamberlain said were "going". were not in an improved state. Poor." Joe." He must have overstepped the mark considerably when he causes C mservative agents to turn and recta him. * * * The deep faith which the Welsh people have in tho new President of the Board of Trade was once some- what infelicitously expressed. Mr. Lloyd• George first made his reputation as a public speaker during the Welsh Disestablishrnent controversy by following a Welsh Bishop round the country and replying to his speeches. Wherever the Bishop went Mr. Lloyd- George went a few nights later. At one of these meet- ings a very earnest Welsh deacon introduced him from the chair in some such terms as these :—" I haff to introduce to you to-night the member for the Carnarvon Borcughs, who hass come here to reply to the misre- presentations of the Bishop of--. in my opinion, gentlemen, that Bishop of —iss one of the biggest liars in creation ; but, thank God—yes, thank God,— we half a match for him to-night." Nobody enjoyed the " bull" more than Mr. Lloyd-George himself. * * *

When Mr. Henry Vivian, the Liberal-Labour candi- date for Birkenhead, visited Warrington we alluded in this column to his great interest in the garden city movement. Mr. Vivian is founder and: honorary •chairman of the Garden City Tenants Limited, a Society which is dealing with the housing of workers. at the Garden City, Letchworth. The Society has already built a number of houses with good results, and feeling that,with the great depression in the build- ing trade, and so many operatives out of work, it would be a good time to push forward with building .operations,s -Mr. Vivian, on behalf of his cainmittee, promised to Imild 100 houses in the next twelve months if those interested in the question would find the money. Mr. Ralph Neville, N.C. at once agreed :to find £2,000, and Lord Brassey £l,OOO, in addition to lending his drawing•room for meetings which Mr. Vivian addressed, explaining his scheme, Other sums have been subscribed. In. a month or two work was started, and by Christmas Mr. Vivian hopes to have over 100 building operatives at work. A. tribute to Mr. Vivian's reputation as a political economist is found in the fact that on Jannary 19th next he will, by special request, read a paper at the Liverpool University to the members of the Liverpool Economic Society.

An op-e-1-1--r-n-ee-th-:ig-;va-S -held in Friar's-gate House, on Thursday evening. Miss Besitick presided. The meeting opened with hymn and prayer, after a song from Miss E. Barnes accompanied by Miss Hibbert, Miss Beswick asked Mrs. Moore the speaker of the evening to give her address. Mrs. Moore referred to the Temperance cause from an individual standpoint. , She told her audience that they only travelled the journey of life once and so they must not let any opportunity go by, in which their influence could be used. Much was being said just now about " Beauti• ful Warrington" but she thought that the "Y "Branch by their lives and example and work were gling to do much more to make it beautiful than by planting trees. After the address, a recitation, and songs were given by Miss Beswick, Miss Hibbert and Miss A. Beswick.

Funeral of the Late Mrs. Francomb. At the Warrington Cemetery on Monday morning the funeral of the late Mrs. Francomb, wife of Alder- man Francomb, of Victoria House, Wilderspool, took place. The officiating clergymen were the Rev. Canon Willis and the Rev- C. A. M. Evans. The chief mourners were Alderman Francomb (husband), Mr. Stanley Francomb and Mr. Bert Francomb (sons),Miss Maud Francomb (daughter),Mr. Alfred Jolley (brother) ' and Mr. T. Jolley (brother-in-law), Dr. Liston, Mr. Samuel Sutton, Mr. W. B. Roberts, Dr. Simpson, and Mr. Ellis, of Winwick. A highly interesting lecture on " Mission Work in China was delivered in St. Barnabas' School on Monday evening by the Rev. F. Jones, of Tai-an-Feu. The lecturer gave a graphic account of the efforts which are being made to Christianise the people in North China and of the perils encountered by the i missionaries during the Boxer rising. A series of lantern views was exhibited and the lecture was much appreciated. PUBLIC 13RNEFIT. UNDER TR& CLOCK FOR BRST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. "Scenes In Many Lands." Brewery Band Concert. _ A crowded audience assembled at the Parr Hall on Thursday evening on the occasion of the annual concert in connection with Messrs. Walker's Brewery Band. An admirable programme had been arranged and the audience throughout showed their hearty appreciation of the efforts of their entertainers. The proceedings opened with the march " The Veterans " (Ca-ter) which was excellently interpreted by the band. Under the conductorship of Mr. P. J. Brady the band also gave Somers ovrrture " Riano," " A hunting scene" (Bucolassi) and " Raminiscences of England" (Godfrey). their efforts being highly successful. Miss Annie Goodwin highly delighted the audience with her singing, and was deservedly encored. A Wescott sang" Once Again " and " The Death of Nelson " with effect, and on each occaion had to make a second appearance. The duet "Ida and Dot Polka" was ably given by Messrs. Peterson and G. Brookes. Mr. A. Grounds contributed the humour of the evening, and his efforts in " Berlesque Oratorio " and a ventriloquial sketch were received with applause. Mr. W. 11. Payton was the aecompanist. The second tree-planting ceremony arranged by the Beautiful Warrington Society took Place on Saturday I afternoon on an open space opposite Victoria Park, which has been placed at the disposal :A the Society until :inch time as it shall be needed. There was a large attendance of those interested in the movement. Dr. Markel formally opened the proceedings, remarking that those present were gathered that afternoon as god-parents, and it was their duty to see that the trees they planted were looked'ovell after during life. Trees were then planted by Mrs. James Fairclough (the first tree), Mrs. Richard Fair- clough, Mrs. C. Harvey Cook, Mrs. F. R. Roberts, the Misses Hough, Mrs. T. Many of the trees had been given, and he Should like to include the givers and the helpers in the vote of thanks.—The resolution was put by Dr Markel, and heartily agreed to.

Conscientious Objection. Forster Jackson, of Stockton Heath, applied at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday for a vaccina- tion exemption order in respect of his twin children. He stated that he had a conscientious objection to vaccination, namely that it would be detrimental to the health of his children. He had consulted many friends and was quite prepared to take any risk.—Mr. Long,the chairman, said: You are a very foolsh man in my opinion. I remember the time when it was a perfect horror to me to walk in the streets of all big towns and see the number of people who were horribly marked through smallpox. The application will be granted. CHRISTMAS PRESENTi.—Dressing-Cases, Umbrellas and Gloves.—J. E. WHITE, 22, Sankey St.—Advt. Trades Council and the L R.C. At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council Which was held on Tuesday, M. Hitchen, in accordance with a notice which he gave at a previous meeting, moved that the Trades Council secede from the Warrington Labour Representation Committee. He did not think the L.R.C. had justified its existence, and he was often at a loss to know whether the Lite. did not mean the I.L.P.—The motion was seconded by Mr. Grealey.—Councillor Stott explained that it was thought desirable that labour should be directly represented upon public bodies. The Trades Council was not in a position to put forth candidates, and a Labour Representation Committee was formed. The Trades Council and the I.L.P. and a number of Trade Unions identified themselves with that body A resolution was passed in favour of a Labour Can- didate for Warrington, and all opposition had come from men of the Liberal Party, who supported Mr. Crosfield. The L.R.C. had not progressed as it should have done, The constitution of the L.R.C. was entirely trade union. Mr. Hitchin said he was fully satisfied with the explanation and withdrew his motion. Mr. Haley, a member of the Local Distress Committee attended the meeting and stated that he was sitting on the Committee as a representative of Labour and would be pleased to receive suggestions from time to time to place before the Committee. Petroleum Oil in barrels anct drums delivered free.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, bridge st., Warring- ton.—Advt.

An effort is being made by the members of the Warrington Tradesmen's Association to bring about an alteration in the date of Warrington's annual chil- dren's festival, and on October 10th they decided to approach the Education Committee with that object in view. At a meeting which was held on Tuesday, it was announced that the Education Committee would at their next meeting have a resolution before them asking that they should fix: Walking Day fn. the Friday before August Monday, and that the school holidays commence on that date.— Pending the decision of the Education Committee nothing further was being done by the Association.— In reply to an application from the Association Mr. Teller wrote stating that the Tramways Committee had resolved to issue halfpenny tokens, for use of the tramcars, to errand boys in 4s. pickets. Mr. W. Roberts who presided over the meetinc,, exnressed his pleasure at the concession, which, he said, he would like to see extended to junior aysitants and appren- tices. LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER TILE CLOCK. BRIDGE-ST R.EET.—Advt. The Rector and Religious Teaching In Council Schools. Preaching on the education question at St. Barnabas' Chnrch on Sunday morning the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) urged as important requirements in the day schools that a sound secular education should be given and also that there should he religious instruction—definite Church teachirm—given in the Church schools every day that the schools were opened by trained and competent teachers Having urged his hearers to do their utmost to maintain Church schools and colleges, the Rector said they must be prepared to press for definite Church teaching to be given to the children of Church parents in the Council or provided schools. They could not force the educational authorities to provide , teachers to give that instruction in the schools pro- vided by the authorities. The first movement must come from the parents who, if they were in earnest on the subject, had only got to make a petition to the educational authority that a time might be appointed and teachers set apart to give that instruction, and it would be given. It was to their immense advantage that they should do so, because if the religious instructioa could be properly given in provided schools they would be saving the expense of providing another maintained school. It would therefore ease the burden if parents would take upon themselves that responsibility. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC takes away that ti red feeling.--(Advt.) Board of Guardians. AN EVANGELIST'S CASE.

Bile Beans Cure Neuralgia.

The praises of Bile Beans have been sounded by the most respectable sections of the community. Praise that will carry great weight comes now from the wife of Mr. Samuel Morris, of Hillside," Guestling, near Hastings, 40 years an Evangelist prominently connected with the Sussex Congregationalists. " For many years I was a great sufferer with my head. The doctors said it was due to chronic neuralgia. There were pains in my face but mostly at the back of my head. I tried many doctors, more medicines than 1 can remember, poultices, and other things, but derivtd benefit. My illness lasted many years, and I really felt that I should never be able to regain my health and strength. The pains lasted for three or four days at a time, and were so bad occasionally that I had to take to bed, being completely helpless ; and my room had to be darkened. When I was ill I was unable to retain food on my stomach, and even rejected water. It was my sister who advised Bile Beans. I had often heard about this medicine, but I was in despair,having tried so many other things. My sister bought a box of the Beans for rue,and I promised to persevere with them. In a very little time I found I was benefiting And my condition improved day by day, until at last I had completely regained my health and become well and strong. My husband says lam like another womar I can now get about and do my work without any .'trouble. I have recommended Bile Beans to several friends and neigh- bours, who all speak in the highest terms as to their efficacy." Beans for Biliousness" make foodJdo von good. convert it into rich, warm, blood, which, in its turn, nourishes the nerves and so banishes all "nerve-aches," such as neuralgia,sciatica, and lumbago. They are a true tonic because they repel influenza and soon brace up the system after a weakening illness. Remember it was the genuine medicine that cured Mrs. Morris, and which will alone cure you; not a spurious imitation or cheap substitute!

THE CLARA BUTT CUNCERT.

The most successful concert of the present season took place in Parr Hall on Wednesday evening, when , Madame Clara Butt's and Mr. Kennerly-Rumford's concert party submitted, through the agency of Messrs. Pearson and Payton Ltd., a most delightful musical programme. It was very gratifying also to note the large measure of public patronage which the concert attracted, for entertainment audiences in Warrington seem to be numerically very uncertain just now. On Wednesday evening, however, the hall was well filled by a 'wanly discriminating public, and the talents of the various artistes were most enthusiastically acknow- ledged. Mr. Kennerley-Rumford and his gifted wife have endeared themselves alike to Warringtonians as to all other musical people, not only because of their vocal accamplishments, but because of the conspicuous ! yet undefinable charm surrounding their personalities 'on the concert platform. Both these distinguished artistes were in excellent voice, and whether as soloists or duettists they gave unqualified delight to the audience. Madame Clara Butt was down on the pro- gramme for three songs which, all of them being vocalised with that consummate taste and intensity of feeling which has made her famous as a public singer were, by means of most determined and vociferous encoring, doubled in number. Nor would the audience take any denial of similar extra levies upon the vocal energies of Mr. Kennerley Munford who certainly reached the high-water mark of the audience's affections in the coupled songs "I think " (Guy ,d' Ilardelot) and " Trottin' to the fair" (C. v. Stanford). The sisters Pauline and Ethel Hook,-by the simple and unaffected charm of their delivery of a number of pretty vocal duets, won well-deserved acclamation, their efforts serving also as a foil for the artistic prowess of Madame Clara Butt herself. Of the two solo instrumentalists the 'cellist, Mr. W. H. Squire, may unhesitatingly be said to have carried off the 'palm. It is some time since M. Tivadar Naclik was last heard in Warrington and in the interval he would seem to have deteriorated both in the quality of his tone and in his technique. The movement from the Mendelssohnian Trio with pianoforte, which opened the eencert, was a dainty piece of executant work all round, and was much enjoyed by an audience who obviously listened to it with unusually concentrated attention. Mr. ,Samuel Liddle is to be highly con- ! gratulated on the musicianly taste and skill shewn in his pianoforte accompaniments.

CHRISTMAS PARC ELS .—The London and North- Western Railway Company have made complete arrangements for the collection, quick transit, and prompt delivery of Christmas parcels in all the principal towns on their system, and all parts of Wales, Scotland and Ireland. Special through vans will be run on the principal trains for the accommodation of the traffic, and additional delivery and collection ser- vices by parcel vans and carts between the receiving othoes and the railway stations will be in operation during Christmas week. The reduced rates which are in operation are in TIO ease higher than the 'atom by parcel post. As an extra precaution, a duplicate address label should be enclosed by senders in each pacLage, so that the name of the consignee may be ascertained in the emit of the ontside address becom- ing detached or defaced. Full particulals as to charges can he obtained at any ot the company's stations and receiving offices.

THE CHARGES AGAINST COUNCILLORS. Investigation Committee and the Attorney-General.

On Wednesday, we understand, Alderman Pierpoint, J.P., acting as chairman of the Investigation Com- mittee, approached the Attorney-General by letter re the charges against two Warrington Town Councillors under the Corrupt Practices Act. The duties of the Committee were alluded to, and the procedure at the Council meeting when the Town Clerk declined to sub- mit his statement of the case was described. Finally, the lettor states that the Committee felt it of the utmost importance that the whole of the evidence should be in the Attorney-General's hands, and a copy of the Committee's proceedings was enclosed. This(Friday)morning Alderman Pierpoint received the following rvly from the Attorney-General's office. Law Offices Department, Royal Courts of Justice, London, December 14th. Sir,—l am directed by the Attorney-General to ack- nowledge the receipt of yonr letter (and enclosures) of the 13th inst. in reference to the charges against two members of the Corporation of Warrington. I am, your obedient servant, William Pierpoint Esq.

JAMES ABBS.

WESLEYAN CHOIR FESTIVAL.

Successful Anniversary.

1 One of tho principil events of the year in connection with Bold-street \ Vesleyan Church took place last week- end when the choir festival was held. In past years the , proceedings attending the celebration have been highly successful and the event was regarded with pleasurable anticipation—which was fully realized. Special services in which the choir largely figured, were held on Sunday. In the morning an address was delivered by the Rev. J. Manning who emphasised the desirability of devotional singing. The preacher in the evening, the Rev. Sidney Pitt, took a similar theme for hisaddress, basing his remarks on "Music and religion." Both services were attended by large congregations. The musical portion of the proceedings was of a special character. In the morning the choir rendered the anthems "Plead thou my cause "and "Come unto me" I the soloists being the Misses Taylor and Sterling, and 1 Messrs. Clayton and Page. Miss Maggie Gandy sang with very pleasing effect "0 Divine Reda.mer." In the evening the anthem was "Be not afraid." At this ser- vice Mrs. A. Booth was heard to advantage in " Hear ye,lsrael "and Mr. Levi Rithardson in the solo "Come unto me." Mr.A.Weber,of Liverpool cathedral choir,was perhaps the principal contributor, his rich bass voice in the solo "Calvary "delighting the congregation. In the afternoon a special musical service was held, at which selections were given by Messrs. Crosfleld's Prize Brass Band, who maintained their high reputa- tion. The band quartette party also contributed to the , 'programme in ccessful manner. Mr. Halliwell acted 'as conductor. . F. Bedford was responsible for a l 1 well-played eup onium solo, and Mr. A. Weber gave expressive renderings of "Thus aaith the Lord," "Behold I stand at the door" and "But who may abide." Mr. John Taylor presided over a large gather- ing. On Monday evening the cantata " The Holy City "was given by the combined choirs of Penketh and Bold-street 1 Churches under the oonductorship of Mr. Frank Hickman. I There was a fairly large attendance which was under the I chairmanship of Mr. R. A. Naylor. The cantata was rendered in an excellent manner, the choir displaying 1 great ability combined with careful training. 1 The choruses in particular were especially well given and the individual contributors also achieved success. Mrs. A. Booth was especially 1 pleasing in her solos and Mr. A. Greenough earned special mention as a tenor of great ability and sweetness of voice. Miss E. Taylor (soprano) and Miss M. Gandy (contralto) were both in excellent voice and highly pleased the audience, while Mr. W. H. Wallington was heard to advantage in the bass solo. A word of praise is due to Mr. F. Hickman for his pains- taking work in training the choir. Mr. G. W. Weiser I presided at the organ and ably discharged his duties. The collections at all the services were in aid of the new organ fund and realized .£4O.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. ----- WEEK ENDING DECENIBER 9th 1905. During the week, the following births and deaths were registered :—Warrington Registration District births, 33, deaths, 2G, The excess of births over deaths was 7.

The birth-rate was equal to 248. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 195 per 1,000 against 182 the average of the previous 6 weeks and against 159 the correspondin,, period of last year. The death rate from the seven chi eezymotic diseases was equal to 30 per 1,000, against 1.5 the average rate of tie previous 6 weeks and against 22 the corresponding period of 1904.

The ages of mortality were:- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 6 3 2 1 9 5 26. The deaths included-3 from whooping conch, 1 from diarrhea, 2 from phthisis, 2 from cancer, 2 from lung diseases, 3 from heart diseases, 13 from all other causes.

The following cases of infectious diseases have been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever 3 cases, phthisis 1 case. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending 9th December, 1905, 40 ; Warrington 21; Newton, 13 ; Rural District, 3. METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 45 Fah. and therefore 3 degs. higher than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 55 F., was recorded on the Bth inst., and the minimum temperature 36 F., on the sth inst.

The rainfall measured .21 inch. The general direction was S.W.

J. GUEST GORNALL. Medical Weer of Health.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR. (Continued from Page C.) NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. STEPHEN H. JENKINS AND J. J. Roctitc.—Regret that it was quite impossible to find space for your letters.— ED. " Ex." Questions for the Candidates.

Sir,—l know there are many voters who would be glad of a definite pronouncement by the two candidates or Parliamentary representation at the forthcoming election, upon the following : lat.—Home Rule for Ireland in view of the appoint- ment of two such avowed Homo Rulers as Lord Aberdeen and Mr. Bryce. 241—For Free Trade, both imports and exports as between different nations I take it there is no divergence of opinion, but the free importation of products from countries that protect their own industries to the exclusion of our own, is one- sided, and works to the disadvantage of many of our own industries. Do the candidates favour a revision of our tariff laws for revenue only or do they favour such alter- ation in the tariff laws as shall lead to preferential trading with those countries who admit our pro- ductions upon an equitable basis and contribute to our imperial taxation? 3rd.—Do they favour a redistribution bill which shall given, fair representation in proportion to popula- tion and taxation ?—Yours faithfully, F. MONKS. December 15th, 1905.

December 11th, 1905 Sir,—Next Tuesday the inhabitants of Wtrrington are tObeaddressed by their sitting representative in Parlia- ment, Robert Pierpoint Esq., soda scion of the nobility in the person of Lord Percy, in view of the near approach of the General Election. The next General Election will not be an ordinary one, in which only the general policy of each of the two great parties in the state will be before the electors for choice. If that were so, it would be purely a party fight. But it will differ from the usual general elections, and be made extraordinary in the sense that a question of policy has been revived apart from ordinary party lines,for electors of both sides will leave them and vote for the maintainance of a policy which is above party, but which has been forced upon them by a prominent politican, and we are to be asked to decide whether it is better for us to retain the Free Trade policy which has been the principal feature of our dealings with the outside world for the last half century, or return primarily to a more modified system of protection such as obtained before the present policy was adopted, but ultimately to heavon knows what. I do not wish to in- dulge in any strong language, but to state the case so mildly that no one can find fault with it, I hope I have succeeded. _ _ _

I take it then that at the meeting called for Tuesday nest Messrs. Lord Percy and Robert Pierpoint and others who will no doubt support them, will present their views of the case to the electorate, and we are in no doubt as to whose policy that view will favcam, for Mr. Pierpo int, who is principally concerned, has declared himself to be a " whole hogger." That is to say that be accepts and will fight for Mr. Chamberlain's outspoken policy in preference to Mr. Balfour's cryptic policy. But I hope that in presenting their views of the case, they will leave no ambiguity, but make clear to all electors what is the measure of Protection they recom- mend, and how its adoption will be better for the people of this country than the Free Trade policynow pursued. For example, the declared object of their recommenda- tion is to pat a duty on corn and thus compel i the foreigner to contribute to our taxation without =teas-

in the price of bread. Will they kindly explain !how this is going to be done, for it appears to many of us , that if a tax is going to be put, upon corn, the tendency I will be for the price of flour, and therefore bread, to be increased at least equal to the tax, and if so, where does the support of the foreigner come in? Again, what reasonable hope can they hold out? I won't, ask them what guarantee they can give, for they connot give any, but what hope can they hold out that these taxes won't be increased?

It is further WCOMmended that an import tax be put upon goods. but only on finished goods, not on raw material. I shall be glad if they will state bow they I will discriminate between raw material and finished goods, and how the taxation of any of these goods, is going to benefit us, as a people. These are a very few of the very many questions emanating from the grect one now before us. But few though they are, a clear and correct answer will help the electorate in the formation of a correct decision.—Yours,

ELECTOR.

Liberal Inconsistencies Sir,—At the social of the Junior Liberal Association on Monday evening, a high moral note was struck by Mr. Gray. He made a plea for a higher moral tone in party politics. This is, assuredly, what every right-minded person would wish to see—but how does this accord with the utterances of our irrepressible friend, Mr. Alderman Pierpoint, at this same gathering ? Speaking of our sitting member, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, he is reported to have said—" As a friend, and as a gentlemen in every sense of the word,he had his best wishes, but as a politician, having imbibed, as be had imbibed to the fullest extent, the pernicious doctrines of Mr. Joseph Chamber- lain, he hardly liked to say he detested Mr. Robert Pierpoint—it was too strong a word—bat, he felt utterly ashamed of him. Mr. Robert Pierpoint might not think he was justified in using such words, but as he was ashamed of the conduct of the Chamberlainites, so he was justified in being ashamed of any man who would do his utmost to assist Mr. Chamber- lain in his nefarious designs." This is lifting party politics to a higher plane of morality with a vengeance. It really makes me des- pair of party politics. Is it not conceivable to Mr. Alderman Pierpoint that on this fiscal question, there are thousands of men equally as intelligent, and, may I say it with whisper- ing humbleness, equally as honest, as lofty- minded, and as tree lovers of their country as be is, who yet differ, and differ most strongly, from him on this question ? I, for one, shall most deeply deplore this question being made the nclasion of party abuse and vilification. There is surely no need to decry the intelligence and the honesty of those who differ from us. Let us in the coming contest treat, and sneak of each other as men who are actuated by the highest motives and a sincere desire for the wellbeiog of our country. And now let me speak of anothvr side of this question, " Protection V. Free Trade," and in what I am going to say I hope I may not be misunderstood. lam not in any sense making an attack upon those whose consistency I impugn. I am merely endeavouring to show their want of con- sistency. Well, we have had speeches from Mr. A. H. Crostield, Dr. Markel and Mr. Henry Roberts—all three strong expoeents of Free Trade. Well now—do these gentlemen really and traly believe in the principles they profess? They say they do, and they tell us how necessary it is for their business—the soap trade—that they should have their raw materials free. To be honest now, has Mr. Chamberlain ever proposed to tax raw 'materials ? I believe not. But having got their raw materials in free how do these gentlemen act in regard to their own bosi- ness ? Do they carry it on an the principles of Free Trade ? I say most emphatically they do not. The soap makers of the country have for years formed an Association—in self protection. They do not sell their produc- tions in a Free Trade sense. They have an agreement by which they bind themselves— under penalties, I believe—not to sell their various classes of foods under certain fixed prices. They also bind themselves as to dig. 'counts and terms of credit. This, assuredly, is riot Free Trade. They fix the prices at which the public mast bay their goods, and also the price 4 at which the tradesmen must sell them. Now this is all ; right, but it is not Free Trade.

My friend, Mr. Henry Hoberte, sells bnild- ing materials, and to protect himself and hie firm he has got the sole agency of many of the goods used by builders. Builders who require these goods are obliged to buy them through him or hie firm. In this case also, prices are fired—there is no competition—no Free Trade. Where does the consistency come in ?

I was speaking the other day to a bedstead manufacturer who told me that some years aco the b,dstead manufacturers of the country had an asstx•iation which fixed prices, discounts, ,tc. Whilst this associatfon held together the business was a paying one— there was money in it—but unfortunately it did not hold together, some of the members broke the agreements, and the association broke up—with what resnit ? A number of the firms in the trade smashed and others have not paid any dividends for years. This, surely, is an object lesson in Free Trade. It certainly seems a curious thing that we should find so many of the leading traders and manufacturers of the country banding them- selves together to protect themselves against the evils of Free Trade amongst themselves and yet that we as a nation should allow the foreigners the free use of our markets—to compete with our own productions without restriction. Bye the bye, what about the Sugar Tax ? We never hear it mentioned by the Liberals now, and yet a few months ago it was ever on their lips. Sugar is again wonderfalTy cheap and yet the bounties are abolished and the tax is still there. Yours. JOHN GANDY. Warrington, Deoember 14th, 1905.

Death of a Local Stationmaster. We regret to announce the death of Mr. John Burrell, for 25 years stationmaster of Padgate, nho passed away on Monday, after a long illness. The deceased who was fifty-eighti years of age was well- known and respected n the district.

" Professionalism a Menace to Sport." At a largely attended " open meeting " of the 'Warrington P.S.A. Society on Sunday. Mr. T. L. Robins, Who presided, speaking of the growth of Trades Unionism, said a pledge should be demanded from every man who joined a Trade Union that he would give only his best to his employer, and that he would have nothing to do with " shoddy" work, as there was quite as much religion in 'doing one's best as there was in preaching a sermon. The Rev. A. Harvey, of Widnes, gave an address on "The Chris- tian Athlete." It would he a sorry day for old England if her youth abandoned outside athletics. Professionalism was a menace to pure sport, and he longed for the old time when football would be played purely for sport and not for" gates." Mr. W. Woods rendered two solos in good style. Mr. R. Wadsworth read the Scripture lesspn, and Mr. R. Nelson and the President, (Mr. E. W: Jones), also took part in the meeting.

Warrington Beat Runcorn.

The return match bet wean Warrington and Runcorn was played at Warrington before 6,000 spectators. Both sides had important absentees, Warrington being without Widdeson and Runcorn without Butterworth. Excellent weather prevailed and a hard-game resulted in a well-merited win by the home side. Teams : Warrington—Knight, back; Jordan,Jones, Isherwood, and Kenyon, threequarter backs ; Hockenhall and Brooks, half backs ; Boardman, Preston, Naylor, Heath, Jolley, Swift, Harmer, and Shugars, forwards. Runcorn—Houghton. back; Moran, If esketh, Harrison, and Deakin, threermarter hicks ; Jolley and Flanagan, half backs ; Walker, Richardson, Padhury, Dixon, Williams, Moulton, Leyland, and Kennedy, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Priestley, Salford. -Touch judges, Messrs. H. Davies, Flixton and F. Ratcliffe, Swinton. Playing with the breeze, lVarrington were soon aggressive, and after Kenyon had made a bold bid on the left, Jolley drove out the invaders, and clever work by Hesketh caused Knight to save. Warrington again attacked through the brilliant play of Boardman, Houghton being twice called upon to save and Brooks was in the act of crossing when stopped by Hesketh. Runcorn then rallied, and Moran was given a chance, being tackled at the corner. The WArrington forwards then got the advantage, and fine passing led up to Kenyon crossing at the corner, Preston missing the goal. Half-time score :—Warrington I try (3 points), Runcorn nil. _ .

Turning round, Warrington again forced the pace,and Kenyon was almost in after tine passing. Jolley drove the invaders out, but lost the advantage through repeated penalties. and returning to the attack Jones made a couple of fine openings, from one of which Jordan scored a try. Knight hit the post with his goal- kick. The issue of the game was never in doubt, and though Moran almost crossed after a strong run, Kenyon easily held the opposition in chec.k.Result:—Warrington 2 tries'(6 points), Runcorn nil.

L.A'NCASIIIRE SENIOR CUP (FINAL). WIGAN DEFEAT LEIGH.

After a pointless iraw a week ago. Wigan and Leigh met again at Wheater's Field, Broughton. on Monday. On the Leigh side Brady took the place of T. Johnson in the three-quarter line, and Watkins took the place of Topping in the Wigan front rank. It seemed almost certain that the interval would arrive with- out any score, but a few minutes before that time Jenkins got possession and passed to Leytham, who scored an unconverted try. Better football MIS displayed after the interval than had hitherto been the case,and Wigan responding to the efforts of their opponents, attacked in turn. Anderson made one brilliant dash which deserved a better fate. hut immedigtely after Jenkins intercepted a pass, and finished up a splendid run by securing a try near the posts. Mason easily placed a goal, and thereafter Wigan pressed to the finish. When the whistle blew Wigan, who were the better team on the day's play, had won by one goal and two tries (8 points) to Leigh nothing

HAPPINESS OR MISERY.

" 'Cie to work, and have such pty As juet koopt life from day to thy In your limbs. . ." SHRUM

: Mr. 11. P. Hughes (" Ae II )it- tll"( has favoured ins with a copy of his "Political Essays and Reviews," which were originally written for and appoared in the Warrington "Observer," under the no 4 de plum! of "A. 0 ineervative Workingman," an! are now i reprinted and pablished in ptmphlet form. This s not the only liter try effusion which I have been pleased to receive from him, and acknowledge, bat this is perhaps the only ocotsioa on which I have I have been tempted to offer some friendly criticism. The present broc4 gre contains many interesting sub- jects, mainly consisting of reviews of some local speakers and speeches, with frank criticisms . and expressions, of opinion on matters discursively and peculiarly characteristic of the 'author. They are, needless to say, examples of that "wide reading" which makes even political essays bright and enter- taining. How Mr. Hughes finds time to get through the amount of work he does really, surprises me, and were it not for the fact of "the midnight oil," I am sure he could not accomplish so much. One of the principle features in his articles, is the pleasant manner in which he approaches his subject and the desire he establishes of touching principles and not individuals. Icm assme him thtt I shall reciprocate that sentiment and shall not, I hope in any way, assume that he has deliber ttely insrepreeented either theme or person. It has been stated that our individual happiness will correspond in a great degree, with the correctness of our opinions, and that the misery Ate endure will bear a proportion to the quantity of error which we have, unintentionally imbibed. It behoves us, there- fore, to endeavour to be correct in our opinions. We know that real reform of nations must first begin in the heart of each man and woman, for it is just in pro- portion as the process of regeneration advances there that external political, social, and religious conditions will become propitious. Galileo said : " Here, at Padua is the principal professor of philosophy whom I have repeatedly and urgently requested to look at the moon - and the planets through my glass, which he pertinaciously refuses to do." We, to-day, think that he of whom Galileo spoke should not have been so bigoted as to : refuse to accept the kiod offer ftr fear, probably, that his ideas of the world might undergo a serious change. So.ne would go farther and would not hesitate to , denounce ch conduct as crass stupidity on the part m j even of professor of philosophy. I a reminded that eve iti : really clever men may, at moments, do j foolish t ngs. The great Newton when at College, jwe are told, had a net in the forma of a cat. Not J desiring to be disturbed in his studies he made a hole at the bottom of his door so that the cat might make its entry and exit unattended. One day a kitten came, so Newton made a little hole at the bottom of the door so that it, too, might come in and go out, for- getting for the moment that the big hole might accommodate both. If any Botlerian reads that yarn be will cry 'chestnuts.' I did, more than once, w‘ten out of hearing of tho Headmaster, who told the tale so repeatedly and with such gusto, when I was a boy at , school. I

Robert Dale Owen, son of the great Hobart Owen,of New Lanark, once wrote : "This dogged line of opplsition—this resolution not to use one's eyes and ears, lest their testimony disturb some preconceived idea, or derange some favourite system —isi equally at variance with philosophy and inimical to improve- ment." In so far as a desire 'to read both sides of the question is concerned, Mr. Hughes and I agree. It may be that enyirontnent and heredity have caused us to arrive at different and divergent conclusions, or that we have been so deeply engrossed with oth& matters that our minds may have become temporarily, warped. In this last attempt of his there are many conclusions at which he arrives that I cannot accept. Some of the arguments he advances to), are T think, I misleading. Hence perhaps, this article. I shall I deal with his references to Chinese labour and the war in South Africa of which he stated "A 'little learning' is a very dangerous thing. At any rate, I found it so, in my case." I cannot enter into other debatable points with him at present. It w mid take too long. The remarks, to which I take exception are to be foend on op. 14 and 15, and read as follows : " When the future historian of our Empire comes to examine into this Chinese labour episode, he may put forward a theory which would perhaps startle our Liberal friends of to-day. if they could I come hack to real the unweloo.ne verdict which posterity may possibly pass upon much that was done, and left undone in South Africa, in the early summer of 1899." As far as can be gathered from a debate in the ' House a little while ago, the present leader of the Liberal party in June, 1899, cloclined—as Leader of the Opposition—to allow the Government—without risking a disaussion in the House—to send out an expeditionary force of 10,000 men to South Africa. Had this been done. perhans the whole course of South African events might hare been materially altered. 1%; ren if War had come later it would have found us prepared with a compact force, or nucleus of an army, on the spot, shortening the struggle and sparing both this country and South Africa much impoverishment of resources. In that case possibly no labour difficulty would have arisen. as the natives would not have had yline to acquire—from the British and Boer Warkthests, for services ant food stuffs—what to many of them has been a practical independency. How easy it is for our Liberal friends to now attack a policy which would, stay be, have never been necessitated but for their own sentimental support of the Boers, who dragged on the war month after month in the vain hope of the Conservatives being ousted and thh Liberals coming in with a policy which wotild have pledged them to repeat Winha. The evil of our insiiicer, party catch-cries is that they first mislead the public into believing something is wrong, &c., &c." The italics are my own. After reading that lengthy statement, I agree with Mr, Hughes that " a 'little learning ' is a very danger- ous thing." The quotations I have made are so unfair (and untrue too), that I am sorry that they should have been introduced into his articles. In this case, at any rate, Mr. Hughes has not allowed himself sufficient time to study the seriousness of such statements; and the wrong impressions which they are calculated to create. The inferences to he drawn from the pare- grape arethat his" Liberal friends " are wrong in their beliefs and that the Liberal Party is to be blamed for the war and its consequences ; that jilt had not been for their own sentimental support of the Boers there would have been no labour difficulty in South Africa —no Chinese Lebour there. It is something new for the Liberal Party to be charged With being responsible for the war. Hitherto, it has always been credited (or debited) with trying to pre- vent it. If Mr. Hughes' statement had been made during the height of the war fever he would probably have bon cheered for making it. To-day, when we have the true history before us we are able to calmly read "facts" which prove conclusively that the war was waged for and on behalf of innocent individuals posing for propriety's sake as capitalists. Men who made, and are still making huge fortunes by compelling others to slavery. Men living in sumptuous places employing others t 3 act as " skittles " to amuse their children. 1 tell you candidly, Mr. Hughes, that this country was made the catspaw of those men ; that we as a nation became a gang of thieves when we snatched from a peace-loving people a land they loved so much: And for what ? Land, gold, slavery ! With British soldiers used .as *butchers to feed the Capitalists' inhuman appetite.

EDNICIND OSBORNE. (Vo be (iontinued).

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

CORRESPONDENTS are particularly requested to send letters intended for Friday's " Examiner " not later than Thursday morning.

The New Governm3nt : A Stuly for Workingmen. is said that things long and anxiously looked for generally find their way to the expectants sooner or later. This homely alage is new being verified in 1 the 0438 of the chiefs of the political organisation courteously recognised as the great Liberal party. For a long time past Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, the leader of that party, and his lieutenants have looked with wistful eyes upon the flesh pots of the house of bondage, or in other words, have hungered for the emoluments and honours of office. That hunger is now being appeased by the promotion of Sir Henry and his friends to power. To predicate what the new legisla- tion will be from newspaper rumours would be unsafe and premature. We roust patiently wait for the King's speech for a revelation upon that mast important point. In the meantime, however, it can do no harm to say that it is believed in many quarters that Home Rule for Ireland will be a leading measure of C.-B. and Co. Well, the principle of Home Rule for Ireland or any place else, is a sound and just one of which no honest na in should be ashamed. In ages long past the Stronglytws and Cromwells of England carried fire and sword into the sister isle, robbed the inhabitants of their independence, and pat a foreign yoke round their necks. And little more than a century ago that cruel wrong was emphasised byan Act of Union so treacherous in its inception and so corrupt in its execution that even the Orangemen—those stalwart defenders of the Crown and constitution, cried shame upon it? The repeal of ' this infamous act, and tho restoration to Ireland of her rights as a nation would be nothing more than acts of justice; and the longer they are withheld the worse it will be for Ireland and for this country as well. But should the C.B. me Isere virility have no more in it than the innocent little bantling of the late lr. Gladstone which left the control of the Irish Customs and Garrison I in the hands of the conquerors, its proper plsce will be the waste paper basket.

But even' genuine Home Rale for Ireland could have no beneficial effect upon the grevions wrongs under which English,lVelith and Scotch workingmen are suffer- ing. in the front milk of these wrongs stands the huge ) Liquor traffic—a Government imposition upon the people to keep them in subjection, and blind their eyes as to their standing and rights as God's creatures. And no real reform of our deplorable social condition will ever be possible while this monster of iniquity is , permitted to stemd in the way. Many years ago ' Charles Buxton, himself a brewer, wrote that " the struggle of the School, the Church, and the Library against the gin palace and the beer house was only another development, of the war between Heaven and hell." (I quote from memory). And so long as forty millions of people are foolish enough to consume one hundred and eighty million pounds worth of intoxicat- I in g liquors yearly the victory is bound to be on the side lof the latter place. When men, unfortunately for them- selves and for society, give way to drink they become as helpless nit children in the hands of land-grabbers and capitalists who f ittea on their ignorance and !impoverishment. A sober, thinking and just people I alone could effectually dell with the land-robbery and the wage-sweating carried on in the mantifantories, mines, and other places. What will the C.-B. Ministry do to prepare the way for such people ? Will they compel the 'brewers and distillers to greatly reduce the volume of their output in order to minimise the terriele consequences of "the trade " ? Or will they follow the example of the quack doctor who, unable to deal with a serious disease, prescribed a harmless nostrum to ' deceive his patient, and content themselves with a little nibbling here and there, the effects of which will be 'felt by the drinker rather than by the drink maker ? I fear the latter will be their policy, for the liquor-men are a great power in the House, and drastic measures against them would hasten the fall of the Government. —Tours truly, December, 1905.

lONA.

P.S.-7-3ince this lettnr was written I have learned that Joh Burns, the Dockers' man, has been given a place in the Caslnet—a cunning and effectual way of closing his mouth about the injustice meted out to the working classes.
