The Great Issue.

Sir,—Mr. A. J. Balfour and his Ministers have now resigned, and the people of this country will soon be called upon to decide on the greatest issue that has been before them for 60 years. Let thete be no mistake' about that issue in the minds of the electors. Let the light of strong English commonsense be brought to bear upon it. to dispel the mists and fogs by which it has been, is, and will be designedly enshrou:ed. About half-a-century ago the last shackles of our trade were struck off, and we were then able to excnange freelyl those goods which we could produce beyond our own requirements, for the goods of other countries which they could produce beyond their requirements, with the result that our foreign trade bounded forward, wages increased rapidly, all kinds of commodities became cheaper, and consequently each shilling went further, and so besides being much better fed we were enabled to indulge our tastes for other things which could not be thought of before, a further consequence of which VMS that home trade vastly increased and the people who had lived under the old conditions , fully appreciated the new. Those who had bean strong advocates for the retention of the old order under which the people groaned, completely turned round, and approved of the new, under which they rejoiced and joined in the general belief that the question of the policy of our foreign trade was settled for ever, as for example the late Lord Bartconsfleld. This was true progress, but now a retrograde step is recommended, and we are advised to go back to the conditions from which our forefathers were resceedaind many plausible falllcies are urged in support of the change. We are told that our increase of imports over exports is ruining our trade and that foreign nations are robbing our people of work, and those who advocate this retroerade step, emblazon their , posters with the words " fore work for the people.' If it had not been stated the inference to be drawn from this is clear, but we have been told that the im- portation of these goods robs the home producers of so much work. I have dealt with this matter before, and no attempt has been made to refute my reasoning. I have shown that the essense of trade is barter, that " is, exchange of goods for goods, that money is valueless in itself and is only a medium by which exchange is effected, that it was introduced solely for the convenience of traders, that any man who makes more of any commodity than he requires, and exchanges his surplus to another man who does require it for so much money, and again exchanges that money for other goods, it may be of a dozen different sorts, in effect engages in barter, but makes money the medium by which it is effected. This mcney is only of value as a medium in the country in which it circulates and is confined each to its own country. No money circulates between one country and another. All goods imported into any country must either be received as a free gilt or paid for. The first part of this proposition cannot be entertained, or if it could, I fail to see how we should be worse oR for it, or why we should seek to stop it. Now the excess of the value of our imports over our exports is about £180,000,000. If this is not given to us we have to pay for it. But how ? It has been said in gold sovereigns. That is impossible for the money in circulation is only sufficient to meet the needs of circulation and cannot be increased or reduced without the interference of an economic law. The money in circulation is not nearly equal to the drain that would be put upon it by such a process. But there is another reason even greater, if such is possible, than the above mentioned which shows that it cannot be done. Many of the goods that we receive, we don't 'produce, and the metal of which money is made is one of. them. We have no mines of the precious metals in sthis country, and weare therefore compelled to import what we need front abroad. It is clear then, that, we do not pay this £180.000,000 in money. If these goods vorth £180,0.00,000 are not given to us, we do not pay far them in money, there is no getting away (yore the fact that they muse be paid for either in goods, services rendered,or received by us as profits on former invest- ments, or, which is more probable all the three. Now how in the name of that commonsense that I previously I mentioned, is a reduction or prohibition of these imports going to produce more work. It is cleqr that if goods imported have to he paid for by goods exported every .Cl.OOO worth of goods imported memos the production of an equal value of goods to pay for them, and therefore of the relative value of work for the people. But if on the other hand, tbe imports are reduced by ,Cl,OOO, goods to the value of £l,OOO less will be required, and. therefore the relative value of work will be lost to the people. But the influence of the gain or loss of that worh will not end there. The loss of the wages that would accrue to them for the relative amount of work in producing that .61,000 worth of goods, will reduce their purchases

of other goods, which they would otherwise get, and proportionately reduce the home demand, awl therefore the home trade, and thus our people would not only have to suffer a loss of work to pay for the foreign imports, but would also suffer loss of work due to the reduction of the home demand.

Another important consideration about this matter is, the ostensible reason or reasons urged for imposing import duties. We bear a great deal about one-sided Free Trade, and are told further that the foreigner is encouraged to send his goods here free, and that he does not contribute anything to our taxes. Now observe, it is urged that if we put an Import tax on foreign goods, we shall keep them out of this country, and find more work for our own people, and that by the same means we shall compel the foreigner to contribute to our taxation. Such a proposition is an insult to our intelligence. It ' is obvious that if either of them is right, they cannot both he right. But is either of them right ? It is im- possible, and if it is not impossible, it is undesirable that we should be debarred from taking some of the things which foreign nations supply, and whatever we get. I have already proved we shall have to pay for with other goods, and in so far receive a benefit. It is im- possible for them to import goods without exporting goods,and it is just as impassible to export goods with- out imnorting goods, so that if we reduce their imports we shall woolly reduce the exports, and therefore re- duce the work for the people, which is the very opposite of what the advocates for an import duty claim. "But, 'Ray these same people," if we don't succeed in keeping foreign productions nut, we at least compel th•ule who send them, to contribute towards our taxation." Do they? Well let us see. No man who has any acquain- tance with foreign trade, I don't care who he is, but knows this to be incorrect, and when he proclaims it, is proclaiming something which he knows to be absolutely untrue. Let the most rated tariff reformer get an inquiry for the price of any goods for delivery in a foreign country duty free. Does he give the price minus the duty? Not a bit of It. lie calculates what it will cost him to deliver the goods where specified, plus the import duo, so that firmPoeing the nrice of the goods without the duty was LIM, and the duty 10 per cent on the value, the importer would have to pay him £llO, be in turn would sell perhaps to the wholesale dealer at £alo plus his profit, which I will suppose is .£ %who would in torn sell to the retail dealer for .£ll6 plus his profit, which I will suppose is about the same rate or £9 155., who in turn again sells to the consumer for .£122 ['is., plus his profits which will suppose is three times the rate of the other two. The consumer therefore has to pay £lll Sc. for what would have cost him at the same rate of profits £126 15s or over £ll more than he would have had to pay without the dnty. it will he seen here that the exporter pays nothing of the tax. hut that the consumer pays it all, and a great deal besides. The effect of this on trade would he enormously against the interests of the people and this is the real issue. If we wish for a continuance of our country's pros- perity, whatever false and opprobrious epithets may he applied to us, W 3 will support that candidate who vill represent Free Trade opinions. If, on the contrary, we • wish for a rapid decadence of our country's prosperity, we will stipnort that candidate who will represent so called Tariff Reform.—Yours faithfully, A. RATFIBONE,

The Mount, Grappenhall.

AGE IS NO BAR.

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People

Cure Old and Young.

Death of Colour-Sergeant Boardman. We regret to record the death of the above-named gentlemen who was son of Mr. Boardman of Earles— town. The Colour-Sergeant,who belonged to to "The Kings " Regiment, was attached to the Egyptian Army, and was well known in Warrington, bqing,prior to the outbreak of the Soudan War, Ore/mastic Instructor at Orford Barracks. After the defeat of the Mandi by the British and Soudanese troops, Mr. Boardman was engaged under Armstrong Boy in the Bahr-el-Ghazal Expedition against the Niam-Niam bes in Central Africa for which he was awarded the Distinguished Conduct Medtl, at the same time being, recommended for arommission. The deceased soldier was Sent at ihe retnest of the Sirdar of the Ecyptian Army to be treated by an eminent English specialist for a complaint contracted on active service, but all efforts to save the gallant soldier's life proved unavail- ing, and he died at St. Bartholomew's Hospital,London, on the 28th ult, at the early age of 31 years.

MY DEAR CRIX.DRENt

No doubt by this tine most of you have com- menced your Christmas vacations, and I hope that all my nieces are busy making some little article for a poor chiid. Remember I have offered very nice prizes for the lest things sent me, and I'm sure you will all have some time to spare during your holidays for a little needlework or crochet, especially if it will help to make other children happy. lam pleased to tell you that I have received many more contributions lately, and, as I said last week, I hope shortly to have the "Children's Corner" composed of tales, poetry., etc., sent by its members. Perhaps it would Interest you to know that we now have six hundred members, and I am still offering post-card albums to any of you Who send me a list of twenty-four nevir names. I have sent one of these albums this week to Thomas Thacker. I received short stories from P. Chatterton, Jessie Allington, Sarah Warburton, and Arthur }-rean, all these members it seems, sent in for the competition, but if they will look at the paper published about a fortnight ago they will see that the prize was awarded to Eric Parkinson. However, I hope you will all try again, as I shall be pleased to give a prize every week if any i of you send me n a really good story or piece of poetry. T. Wills sent me a very interesting and well written essay on "Formby," but it is rather too long to print. You will see I have given some puzzles below, these are not very difficult, so I hope you will all try and solve them, I shall award a prize to the sender of the first correct and neatest solution sent me. . _

I must say good-bye for this week and I hope to hear from you all during your holidays, With all good wishes from, UNCLE NED.

MY DEAR U.:%ILE NED,

I am now writing and telling you where I live and how I spend most of my time. I live in the country, and when I come home from school, have and had my dinner I see to my two brothers and help to get them, ready to return back to school, then when I come home again we have our tea. When my brothers are gone to bed, I sometimes make my dolls acme clothes, and some nights I play with my little black kitten which I call Tiny, and then some nights I put the kings and queens of England together in little block7s. When we have a holiday from school I play with the other little children about my own age, and - sometimes go out to see friends, and one lady has given me a very nice book, which is called "Time Chip Boy." Now, I see in one of your letters, that you would like to see a photo of your neices, so I have enclosed a post-card with myself on, and my little brother standing by the boarded fence. . .

Hoping you will accept it, I remain your loving neice, ALICE MAY MITCHELL.

DEAR UNCLE NED,

It gives me great pleasure to read the " Chil- dren's Corner," and to see what a great number of nephews and nieces you have got, and I should like to be in your League, and be one of your nephews. I think it would be a good idea to have your photo put in the " Children's Cor- ner• sometimes, it would give all nephews and . pieces a chance of seeing their Uncle Ned,' The evenings are very dark now, and I enjoy myself by trying to solve the puzzles. My brother has sent to Hovis & Co. for one of their puzzles, but it has not come yet. There is not anything else I can tell you, I must now conclude with best love. Fromyour affectionate nephew,

THOMAS ORAM.

DEAR UNCLE NED, Lam fourteen years old. I have never • seen a letter from Barrow in your " Letter-box," so I thought I would write to you. My favolite sports are cricket and football. I enjoy reading the letters and stories. hor my summer holidays I went to the Isle of Man and the Windermere lakes. lam learning to play the violin, and like it very much. I remain, your friend and reader, HARRY T---.

CHARLIE AND THE HEN.

Charlie was twelve ,years old; his brother Johnny was two years younger. Johnny Was a sturdy little fellow," and Charlie was not always mindful of the two years' difference in their ages. One morning, in the early winter, the little boys were warming their hands over the stove, when their mother said : "Johnny, I wish you would go to the barn and see if 'Old Speckle' is on her nest again. I do not wish her to set this winter, for the little chickens would freeze to death. If she is on her nest, I wish you Would lift her off, and drive her out into the barnyard." Johnny went to the barn, and found old Speckle on her nest in the hay-mow. He climbed up tne 'ander, and put out his hand to take her from ner nest. Old Speckle did not like this. She said, " Cluck! cluck!" and ruffled up her feathers and tried to peck Johnny's hands.

Then Johnny took off his hat and waved it at her, and said,. " Shoo! shoo! shoo ! " but old Speckle would not leave her warm nest for Johnny; so Johnny went into the house, and told his mother he could not drive old Speckle off, and he was afraid to take her up in his hands.

" Oho !" said his brother Charlie, laughing at him. "Before I'd be afraid of a hen ! " Well," said Johnny, "I don't deny it, and if you are not afraid of old Speckle, I should like to see you take her off yourself." "You will see old Speckle in the barnyard in less than five minutes," said Charlie, as he took his hat and went out.

Before long, the people in the house heard a loud cackling like that of a very angry hen. "That must be Speckle," said Johnny's mother. "I suppose Charlie has taken her, off the nest. Be is a brave boy. Old Speckle is a fierce hen." Then Charlie came in.

"Do you hear that hen?" said Charlie. "I told you I could take her off from her nest. I'm not afraid of a hen."

Then Johnny, who had been out too, spoke up and said:

"'Most anybody could rake a hen off a nest "Rake a hen off a nest?" repeated Charlie, laughing, but looking sheepish. "How do you know I did? "

Then Johnny told how he knew. The barn had both a back door and front door. The back door was kept open, and the front door was kept closed. As soon as Charlie had left the house, Johnny slipped out of the house door and in at the back door of the barn. He hid in the hay before Charlie had opened the front door of the barn. He saw Charlie climb the ladder, and saw him wave his hat at old Speckle, and say, " Shoo, Speckle, shoo ! " He ' saw Charlie try to take old Speckle off, but she pecked at him so defiantly that Charlie was afraid to touch her. So he took a long- handled rake, and reached over to old Speckle and raked her, away from her nest, as if she had been a bundle of hay. Old Speckle still fought pluckily for the possession of the nest, and thrust her head between the prongs of the rake in her efforts to reach the eggs. It seemed almost cruel in Charlie to drag her farther away from them, but, as he only pulled steadily, it did not hurt her in the least. But she was soon convinced that it was useless to struggle, and so she flew down. on the barn floor, and ran out at the door, cacklinn, an indignant " Cut ! Cut! Curdar-cut ,as loudly as she could. Charlie went out after her, and while he 'stopped to fasten the door, Johnny ran out at the back floor and into the house. After this, when Charlie would accuse Johnny of being afraid of anything, Johnny would answer, " Let me see, I believe I remember you. Aren't you the boy who raked the hen off her nest?" But when Johnny's mother heard this taunt, she quietly remarked, "It is. not every boy who would think of as good a plan as Charlie's."

Popular Penny Concert. Another exceedingly enjoyable entertainment was given in 80ld-street schools on Saturday evening, the room being filled. Songs were rendered in. a highly ! creditable manner byMiss Garnett Ball, Mrs. Coulton and Mr. Clay, and a duet tiv the Misses Pigott was well received.. Miss Cissie-Pigot gave a pianoforte solo and a zylophone solo by Miss Winifred l'igot, proved popular features. Recitations and a mono- lor.tue by Miss Amy Pigot were given with fine effect. A fitting concluding !item was a quar- tette by Miss Woodland, Miss Fairhurst, Mr. Corrin and Mr. Clay, " the 100th Psalm" (set to music by Mr. T. Pemberton, an inmate of the War- rington Workhouse.) The audience throughout was keenly appreciative and' numerous !encores were accorded. Mr.J.Moore Murray presided and delivered a short address in which he laid stress upon the advantages of the children occupying. some of their time in the winter evenings in practising their school songs and interesting themselves in drawing.,

THE CURRANT BOOKLET.

Amongst the good cheer which will contribute all over the world to make the coming Christmas merry, greater importance should be attached to currants, the delicious little dried berries coming to us from Greece. They are abundant in nutrition, and rich in certain acids which are most %minable aids to health and strength. There are many methods of cooking currants, and num- bers of delicious dishes suitable for the; festive season can be made from the fruit. Your grocer baK a supply of small booklets giving various recipes for preparing the fruit, and you can have it free of charge. You should know that there is a special variety of currants sent from Vostizza in Greece,which are used as dessert. They are delicious eaten alone,and also a suitable accom- paniment to almonds or grated cocoanut. Everyone wishful to add a wholesome, welcome and economical delicacy to the table this Yuletide should make a point of asking the grocer for the little book.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The Boarding out of Children.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board o f Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present :—Messrs. A. R. Norman (presiding), C. Roylance, A. Greenway, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, G. Egerton ‘Varburton, J. Whitlow, A. Broom, C. E. ',maker, J. H. Davies, J. Smith, T. Wright, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, J. Pilling, S, Horton. J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, J. Fryer, J. Spruce, S. Taylor, W. M. Morris, P. Collins, J. Douglas, J. W. Temperley, J. Rutter, J. Hallows, J. Whitley, R. Billington. C. E. Linaker' jun., J. B. Chadwick, T. Antwis, G. F. Ashton, (clerk) W. E. Hough, (assistant clerk), Cook de Prez and Person (re- lieving officers), and J. Cunnah (Workhouse master.) THE BOARDING OUT OF tHILDREN. QUESTION AGAIN ADJOURNED. The Clerk in accordance with instructions presented a tabulated statement of the cost of the maintainence children in the Workhouse. He found that boys cost in food 2s. VA., clothing Is. 31d., other necessaries 10d. total 4s. 82d.-per week, girls, food, 2s. VA., clothing Is. 2d., other necessaries 10d., total 4s. 71d: In addition establishment charges had to be added which without reckoning anything for officers salaries brought the total cost per week to boys Gs. 8:1c1., girls, Gs. 71d. "It is to be distinctly understood continued the report " that if the children were moved from the workhouse the whole of the principal and interest and a large proportion of the necessaries such as furniture repairs and establishment charges would still have to be borne for the upkeep of the Workhouse. As the the above figures are much in excess of the amount which was generally considered to be the cost of maintenance in the Workhouse I have examined the books to ascertain the cost per bead per week exclusive of officers' salaries of all classes in the House and I find that taking in all the above-mentioned items the cost averaged during the four years ending Michaelmas,ll/04, was Gs. 4d. per week. If the cost of officers' salaries is included tne cost is Ss. 2ld. per week. I have also referred to Mr. D-ancy's report, but it is to be understood that Mr. Dancy only dealt with those items which were actually charge- able to in-maintenance and that no account was taken of the other items which I have mentioned such as furniture repairs, establishment charges, &c. I com- pared the cost in the Runcorn Union with the cost in other Unions and taking the figures from Mr. Disney's return I find that the cost per week in Cheshire Unions so far as items actually chargeable to in-maintenance ace concerned, varies from 4s. to 4s. 11d, per week. It will be seen that the cost in the Runcorn Workhouse compares favourably with that in other Unions. The Guardians may, I think assume that the other expenses are equally reasonable." The question was further adjourned to enable the specially appointed committee to consider the report.

THE VISITING OF CHILDREN.

Mr. Roylance said ha thought children who went from the Workouse should be occasionally visited by the Master and the Matron, or the relieving officers. Ile moved that it be an instruction to those officers that all children who left the Workhouse be periodically visited. After some discussion Mr. Cunnah in reply to the Chairman, said be end the Matron kept in touch with all the children who left the Workhouse. The Board expressed their satisfaction with the Master's assurance and the matter was allowed to drop.

COMMERCIAL FATLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial Failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, December Bth, was 187, viz. New bankruptcy proceedings published in the London Gazette, 91; deeds of arrangement registered, 96. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 96; deeds of -arrangement 75—total 171 ; being an increme of 16. The total number of commercial failures recorded during the 49 weeks of the present year is 8,518 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 49 weeks of last year was B,4l7,showing an increase of 101.

hard as we can, but we must not hit below the belt. (Hear, hear.) We'7must treat our opponents in such a manner that,however keenly we may fight,we will never sacrifice their personal friendship. I say this very feelingly because all my rersonal friends belong to the Tory party—(No,no)—at least the great majority of my personal friends belong to the Tory party—and although they have always credited me with being every hard fighter and doing the best I can on behalf of Liberalism, they are very glad to see me whenever I go amongst them, and I attribute that to the fact that I always fight fairly (Cheers.) I want to fight fairly to-night and on behalf of the Liberal party in Warrington. I would ask you for a moment to consider the Government,which,fortun- ately, we have at last got rid of—or at all events they have got rid of themselves. Fear or shame, one or the other, has compelled them to return to that obscurity from which I wish they had never emerged. (Cheers.) You all know what their policy has been and you all know what their policy would have been had they continued in office. I think it was shame that took them uway—(cheers)—and we ought to be, and I know we are glad that we at last have the opportunity of serving under a Liberal Government. (Cheers.) If there is one thing in the formation of this Govern- ment that pleases me more than another it is the fact that you now have direct representation of labour— (cheers)—in the person of a personal friend of my own —Mr. John Burns (cheers.) Ho is a man who at one time worked for less than 30s. per week—(hear, hear and laughter)—but His Majesty acknowledges that he is endowed with sufficient brains to take charge of a most important Government Department. (Cheers.) I will not detain you very long and will come at once to the object for which I rose, the proposing of the name of one—s name which has always been dearly loved in ' Warrington for the munificence and kindly work which its bearers have always carried on—the name of Crosfield, which is a household word in the town— (cheers)—for unostentatious charity. There is no one who knew Mr. John Crosfield better than I did and I am able to tell you that in the many cases that I had to appeal to him on behalf of what I term charit- able objects, Mr. Crosfield at once asked me what I thought he ought to give and the money was there. (Cheers.) The name of Crosfield, in my opinion,is synonimous with good work in Warring- ton—(cheers)—and as that applies to the old members ' of the family more especially,so I think their mantle has descended upon the younger generation. In my opinion you have in Arthur Crostield a gentlematfwho is almost perfect as a politician. I believe he is quite prepared to go as far as I would in the interest of labour. (Cheers.) lam sure he would satisfy all of us in the %ray of economical government. and I believe he is as true a patriot as any imperialistic jingo in the land. (Cheers.) To my mind he is exactly the man that Warrington wants as a Liberal representative—(cheers) —and I am sure he fills your minds in the same respect. (Cheers.) I need not hesitate and I have the greatest pleasure in proposing that Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield is a fit and proper person to become our candidate. (Pro- longed cheers.)

Crosfield is a fit and proper person to become our candidate in the forthcoming election, and if he accepts, this meeting hereby pledges itself to use every legitimate means to secure his triumphant return." (Cheers).

THE CANDIDATES RECEPTION. The resolution was put to the meeting and carried unanimously and with great enthusiasm.

Alderman PIERPOINT and Mr. ELWELL then left the room and in a few minutes returned with the adopted candidate. As Mr. CROSFIELD entered the room the audience rose and after cheering continuously for some moments sang" For he's a jolly good fellow." A scene of the greatest enthusiasm prevailed. Upon silence being again restored, Mr. PA RICER Turned to Mr. Crosfleld and said : I can assure you that the promise which has been made in the resolution passed by this meeting will be fulfilled. We have asked you to undertake ft very arduous task, but we feel you will be sustained in the effort by a united party. (Cheers.) We feel sure that the flag of Liberalism and the flag of Free Trade will be safe in your hands. If you will hold that (lam on high, and whether it be to victory, or whether it to defeat—if we are going to think of that at all—" No, no ")—we know that tint banner in your hands will not be soiled. I have pleasure in conveying to you this resolution, and in asking you to honour us by becoming our candidate. (E'rolonged cheors). ' - -

WARRINGTON'S LIBERAL CANDIDATE.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELO UNANIMOUSLY

SELECTED.

ENTHUSIASTIC MEETING AT THE LIBERAL

CLUB.

Speeches by Mr. A. H. Crostield, Alderman Plerpoint, Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. J. Elwell, Rev. John Yonge and Others. A meeting which will rank as a memorable one in the history of the Liberal party in Warrington was held at the Liberal Club on Wednesday evening, the object being to select a candidate for the forthcoming general election. The assembly had been convened hy the Liberal Association and so large was the attendance that the usual meeting room upstairs proved inadequate to accommodate rill who came and an adjournment was made to the billiard nom. Remarkable enthusiasm characterized the proceedings throughout and a resolution was unanimously adopted declaring Mr. A. H. Crostield to be a fit and proper person ' to again contest the seat and pledging the support of the meeting on his behalf. Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the Liberal Association) presided and the attendance included all the best-known work- ers and supporters of the Liberal cause in Warring- ton. It was a thoroughly representative and earnest gathering. ALDERMAN TINNION AND UNITY.

Mr. C. JAcKsots HOLMES said he had received a telegram from Alderman Tinnicn, who was in London, in which he said" Regret T oannot join you. Heartily support the adoption of Mr. Crosfield. With present enthusiastic unity of Party victory certain." (Applause). Re had also received a letter from Mr. Patrick O'Brien, chairman of the Town Hall Ward, who expressed his regret at his inability to be present. He said in his letter " You have no other and no better choice than to ask Mr. Crosfteld to become the candidate, because of his earnest, honest, end consistent advocacy of and work and sacrifice for Liberalism." (Applause). Mr. PARKER, who was received with applause, said they were all acquainted with the object of the meeting, and it was in the hands of the gentlemen representing the Liberal Association, to select a candidate to tight in their interests at the next General Election. (Applause.) ALDERMAN PIERPOTNT PROPOSES MR. ' CROSFIELD. _

There was silence for a minute or two followed by a sodden and prolonged outburst of applause as Alderman Pierpoint rose to his feet to address the

gathering. Alderman PiERPOINT said: Let me thank you for the cordiality of the greeting which you have given me, and which brings back the very kindly feelings which have always existed between myself and the great body of workers amongst the Liberal party. (Applause). Nine has not been a "moonshiny" kind of connection with the party but has been a long and I hope a consis*- ent one. I have always endeavoured to impress upon the Liberal party that we should always fight earnestly and thoroughly, and should observe the rules of fair play. Let us hit our political opponents as

MR. CROSFIELD AND LABOUR.

Mr. ELWELL who was received with enthusiasm said: As a working man I have great pleasure in seconding the proposition. I consider the present to be more a workingman's fight than any during the last half century. (Cheers.) We have seen during the past two or three years statesmen in this country who have been doing their utmost to bring about the reversal of that policy, entered into 50 or 60 years ago,which has proved of such lasting benefit to the workingmen of this country. We have also seen their muddling in labour questions in South Africa where they did their best to assist employers to obtain cheap labour. A Govern- ment, 1 think, should be strictly impartial and any Government which assisti any employers to a position of advantage over the workingman, or assists the work- ingman to take an advantage over the employers is not acting fairly. (Cheers.) Another thing connected with

(Loud cheers). REV. JOHN YONGE AND THE NEED FOR UNITY The Rev. JOHN YONGE, who was greeted with applause, said the meeting brought back recollections of 1880 when he fought under the leadership of Mr. John Gordon MelWinnies. He had felt very disappointed that Liberalism in Warrington had had to take the second place which it had occupied for so many years. He hoped and liked to believe that the day had come when the tide had turned in Warrington. (Cheers.)l They had a stubborn enemy to fight and a big majority to overcome but he believed that if every man amongst them did his duty and his level best, victory was not an impossible thing. (Hear, hear.) He thought the battle was more the battle of the private soldier. They must not rely upon the leaders however distinguished and influential they might be but every man and he was almost going to say every woman—(hear, hear)—must do their duty, and see that Liberalism was raised from that position which it had unhappily held for so long. He was perfectly sure that in their candi- date they had a man who would bo an honour to their cause, of whom they had every reason to be proud, and who, they might expect, when he got to the House of Commons would make his Way and obtain a position of influence and honour amongst the statesmen of the day. (Cheers). They all rejoiced at the excellent Govern- ment which had been constructed. He confessed that he was a little bit afraid of what would be done but when he saw the names of the gentlemen appointed to the Cabinet he had the impudence to think he could not have done it better himself. (Laughter and cheers.) Every man in that Cabinet commended himself to his judgment, and he rejoiced as much as any working mars there to see Mr. John Burns in an important office. In his opinion Mr. Burns had done everything to deserve his elevation, and he rejoiced to know that he was held in honour by all reasonable and intelligent people, from the King downwards. (Hear, hear.) He was pleased to be present, and assure Mr. Crostleld and all who cared to be assured, that he was entirely with the Liberal party, and he hoped and trusted and would pray that by the Divine blessing that triumph might be achieved which they all desired. (Cheers.) OTHER SPEECHES. Mr. GEORGE WALLINGTON: said that at the present time the Liberal party in Warrington had their oppor- tunity and it was necessary that they should all put their shoulders to the wheal and do their level best. The party stood for peace, retrenchment and many reforms and if the people wanted those reforms they would have to do all that lay in their power to support the party. (Cheers.) Councillor JAMES EVANS said he was very pleased that Mr. Crosfield had again accepted the position. He did not think they had forgotten the spirit with which he accepted the invitation to fight at the last election. He thought that considering he did so well under such adverse conditions as prevailed at the last general elec- tion there would be a different counting of votes when the poll was next taken. (Cheers.) He knew a little about the electorate of Warrington and he did not think he had ever seen a better feeling manifested, and he believed that if they did their duty and steadily put their backs into the fight they would have the satisfac- tion of knowing that once again Warrington would be represented in the House of Commens by a good, honest and substantial Liberal. (Cheers). Mr. E. J. GUEST said that for a considerable time we had had a Government who had not been the servants of the people but the servants of monopoly.(Hear,hear.) They had been the servants of the Anglican Church and had passed the Education Act, which was largely in the interests of that Church without giving sufficient con- sideration to Nonconformists. In their Licensing Act the Government had thwarted the magistrates in the direction of reducing the number of licences in the country. (Cheers.) The Government desired to be theservants of the rich and he thought the whole idea of their fiscal policy was to make the rich richer without any regard to the poor, though they would not be able to accomplish that. (Hear, hear.) In their candidate they had a gentleman who did all he could to further the interests of the people and therefore he thought they should all give him their hearty support and hoped any minor differ- ences if they had any would all be put on one side. 1 (Cheers.) Mr. J. MORTON said he believed all shades of labour would go strongly for Mr. Crosfield, not only the labour represented by the Progressive Labour League but those sections which did not recognize the Liberal party. (Hear, hear.) He thought the enthusiasm which had been displayed that night augured well f)r their success at the coming election. At auguredthe same time they must not go into the battle feeling that to obtain the victory was an easy matter. (Cheers.) They wanted to win, but they must , not only go in for winning, but they must go in for so large a majority that anyone who came forward in the future would stand no possible chance of planting a Protection scheme. WOMEN LIBERALS' "AT HOME." Successful Function at the Parr Hall. The Parr Hall was the scene on Tuesday evening of one of the most successful social functions ever held in connection with the Liberal party in Warringtoa. It was a happy thonglit which prompted the active workers of the Women's Liberal Association to arrange an " At Home," for it gave many local Liberals an opportunity of coming more closely into touch with each other. It was also an admirable way of expending the proceeds of the recent sale of work held at the club. Invitations were issued to members of the Association and each lady had the privilege of inviting a gentleman friend. There was a gratifyingly large response, the attendance nu laboring about 1,000. The hall had been very tastefully decorated by the ladies' committee, the body of th,. building being transformed into a drawing room. Plants had been hired from the Town Hall and consid- erably improved the effect. 'The guests were received by Miss Crosfield, who acted as hostess, and Mrs. C. E. Parker. Shortly after seven o'clock Mr. A. H. Cros- field entered and was warmly greeted, and he also assisted in receiving the visitors. A highly enjoyable musical programs was submitted, the arrange-. ments being in the hands of Mrs. C. Jankson Holmes, (hon. secretary of the Associati on). Songs were very pleasingly rendered by Miss Marie Houghton (soprano), Miss Barnes (contralto), and Mr J. Willett, (tenor). Selections by the Apollo Quartette party were well received. Mrs. C. Jackson. Holmes acted as accompanist and gave pianoforte solos which were much appreciated. A cleverly executed pianoforte duet by Mrs. Holmes and Miss Crosfield forned one of the most acceptable items, and Miss Cros- field was responsible for a solo on the same instrument which gave much pleasure to the audience. During the evening light refreshments were served, the catering being undertaken by the committee. There was a short interval for speech-making. Mr. C. E. Parker presided and expressed the pleasure it afforded the assembly to see Mr. and Miss Crosfiele present. Mr. Crostleld, he said, was a Warrington man and they know his sterling worth. They knew he was an ardent Liberal, that his convictions were well formed, and that he was thoroughly sound on all the great ques- tions of the day. He was a thorough-going Free Trader --(applause)—and was in sympathy with all the upward movements of humanity for which the Liberal party was striving. (Applause.) Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who was received with great enthusiasio, thanked the gatkering for the great cordiality and kindness expressed in their welcome. Continuing, he said, that as the meeting afforded him the opportunity of speaking to a great number of their lady friends,there was one point in the great controversy in which they were engaged that he would like to try and illustrate, because It was a point that went home to everyone. Personally he was very thankful that although in some aspects the Free Trade controversy was complex, yet in its most important features it was a controversy which they could all understand and appreciate. He thought perhaps the ladies could appre- ciate it more than anyone else. They knew that it was ' proposed to put a ten per cent. duty upon all manu- factured goods coming into this country. That meant that everything, roughly speaking, would be 2s. in the .0 dearer ; for other reasons probably a little more than 23. But taking it on the 2s. estimate, that meant that the wage of a sovereign would only have the purchasing power of 18s., and what followed? They knew that the first necessaries in a working man's cottage were the food that had to be bought and the rent that had to be paid. What, therefore,was knocked off in the weekly budget first ? If they might take a lesson from Germany they would find that woollen goods were the first articles that were not purchased so freely with the margin at the disposal of the wage . earner. After woollen goods they found that boots were the next ' articles that suffered. He asked his hearers to consider how that affected the question of the unemployed. Fvery one in that room could understand that if fewer woollen goods were bought, and if a pair of shoes had to be knocked off in the year by the 40,000,000 people in these islands the manufacturer would not be kept so busy. (Applause). They always heard a great deal about foreign trade, but they must remember that the home trade was six or eight times bigger than the foreign trade rind all of them could understand that if the demand fell off, and there was a greater slackness in trade,in demand and in tuaufaeture, then the capitalist would begin to say "There are so many men outside with- out a job,can't we consider a reduction of wages?"( Hear, hear.) He asked them all to bear that in mind, and then they would all appreciate at their full value those rash and insincere promises that were made to catch votes, namely the promises to cure the evil of the unemployeil by finding more work for English workpeople and that at higher wages. The thing was so patent that one wondered that a man of Mr. Chamberlain's perspicacity and ability should dare to try to gull the British public with nonsense of that kind. (Laughter and applause.) For his (the speaker's) own part he could only think it was the result of the success with which Mr. Chamber- lain had found be was able, unfortunately, to gull so many people when the war fever WEIS abroad. Mr. Chamberlain would find that it was one thing to mislead public opinion heated in time of war and quite anotherthing to persuade hard-headed English folk that two and two made five in time of peace. (Hear, hear and laughter.) He was not going to do it, but know- ing as he did that he could not carry the question at this election, Mr. Chamberlain had candidly told them that he counted on carrying it at the next one. That was why it was so important that all sensible people should see that this chimera of Protec- tion was knocked on the head at the next election. (Applause.) He (Hr. Crosfield) had heard friends of his say "Protection has not a look in. England will not listen to it. It does not matter what Warrington does." But it did matter. (Applause.) They had to teach the Conservative Party, or rather the Protectionist Party because a great many sensible Conservatives were going to be with them on this occasion—(spplause)—and, if he might refer to that homely animal with which they

===== 0002601_19051216_art0118.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
•

===== 0002601_19051216_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
were all familiar, they had to teach their friends the whole-hoggers that they were on the wrong tack, and that it was no use bringing the question up again at a second election, because English people were too wise to listen to it. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He hoped the election would be fought out with deter- mination and courage and, might he add, with the same cheery, genial, and good spirit which evidently animated the delightful gathering that night. (Applause) What would be the issue of the party council that was to be held on the following evening he did not know, but when the decision was arrived at and if it e.hould be that he had a duty to perform, they might be certain that he would do it to the best of his poor ability in as whole-hearted a manner as he ever fought any battle, commercial or political, in his life. On the eve of the great events that would make hi4tory in the course of the next month he could but thank all present moat cordially and respectfully on behalf of his sister and himself for attending that evening in such great num- bers and being such an attentive and appreciative audi- ence. (Applause.) Councillor MAGI-Ns briefly addressed the gathering and thanked Mr. Crosfield for his interesting and com- prehensive speech on the approaching contest. There never was a time in the history of this country when women's votes were more needed to carry on the work of the nation than at the present moment. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The late Government never were the friends of the working people. They had drained every means of raising taxation and now they wanted still greater liberties to tax the people even to the extent of 2s. in the 4. If Mr. Chamberlain's proposals were carried out what would happen ? Mr. Crosfield had said that the sovereign would shrink to 135., but he (Councillor Maginn) believed it would shrink as low as 15s. When bread was dear in the old times W tges were not higher and he would ask his hearers to resist the polio,- which would raise the prices of commodities. The CIIATRNIAN said he was sure Councillor Maginn had voiced the feeling of all present. They all very much appreciated the kindness of Miss Crosfield in ask- ing them to be present. They looked upon her co- operation and help in the forthcoming election as a very valuable asset, and the ladies of , Warrington led by Mrs. Armitage, Miss Dutton and Miss Crosfield would be very useful in the important, work to be carried out. (Applause.) The proceedings throughout were most successful and enjoyable. Amongst those who attended during the evening were :—Alderm in Pierpoint, Mrs. Gridths, Mrs. Robert Garnett, Mr. Philip Garnett, Councillor and Miss Evans, Councillor and Mrs. Clegg, Councillor and Mrs. Maginn' Mr. W. Madden, Mr. and Mrs. W. Furness, Mr. and Mrs. F. R. Roberts' Mr. and Mrs. E. Osborne Mr. E. J. Guest and Miss Guest, Miss Bassie Dutton:Miss Gornall, Hr. E. V. Walker, Miss ‘tialker. Miss Dodds, Mr. and Mrs. J. Howes, Mr. and Mrs. T. Barnes. Mr. J. Elwell, Mr. J. Morton and Mr. J. Howes, jun.

JUNIOR LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. Successful Social Gathering. SPEECHES BY MR. C. E. PARKER

ALDERMAN PIERPOINT AND OTHERS. The recent formation of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association was hailed with satisfaction by well wishers of the party, and the organization promises to fully justify its existence during the corn- ing campaign. Much has already been done to interest young Liberals in political work and there is a strong and active membership. On Monday evening a highly successful social gathering in connection with the Association was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe. The function had been arranged with the object of giving the members and their friends an opportunity of meet- ing Mr. A. H. Crest'sld but to the regret of all, the 1 prospective candidate was unable to be pre- sent as he was suffering from a sore throat. A large company sat chwn to tea, which was, excellently served, and afterwards a concert was held. Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the Liberal Association) presided and amongst those present were :—Ars. Parker, Alderman Pierpoint, Rev. J. L. Milne and Mrs. Milne, Rev. C. Harvey Cook and Mrs. Cook, Councillor Ellison, Councillor Evans and Miss Evans, Councillor Barton and Mrs. Barton, Councillor Burton, Mr. and Mrs. W. Furness, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes Mr. and Mrs. W. Roberts, Mr. and Mrs. A. Rathtone, Mr. and Mrs. J. G. Withinshaw, Mr. and Mts. Joseph Howes, Mr. and ' Mrs. T. Barnes, Mr. and Mrs. E. Osborne, Messrs. Harold Gray (secretary), J.Morton, J. Howes, jun.,J. Hawthorn, H. W. Guest and A. Tilling. Apologies for non-attend- ance had been received from Alderman Tinnion Councillor Brough, Mr. H. Roberts, Mr. F. R. Roberts, Mr. W. Madden, Councillor Wilkinson and Councillor Peacock. A most enjoyable musical programme had been arranged and was fully appreciated. Miss Fairhurst gave an artistic interpretation of "The Raft " and Mr. Gittens sang " The Veteran's song" with fine spirit. The Apollo Quartette party ( Messrs. Walker,Critchley,Woodcock and Cook) rendered Anchored " and "The old Kentucky home" very effectively. Mr. W. J. Ford contributed a clarionette solo in creditable style and Mr. E. A. Sanders delighted the assembly with a humorous sketch for which he was heartily encored. Mr.J.Forster acted as accompanist and also gave a pianoforte solo. During the evening there was some interesting speech- making,

Mr. H. CIRAY (secretary) submitted a review of the work of the Association. He said the aims of the I Association were educational and moral. Many of them felt that in their midst were many young, ardent, and progressive spirits, but that they were largely I moving in quite isolated spheres. All were deeply con- scious of the wrongs being inflicted upon the country; I Flow could they do their share in abating these evils . was the question. Unity, they instinctively felt, was essential. There was a verse which ran When bad men combine, Good men must agree. (Applause). To co-operate would be to strengthen their cause ; hence they combined, and felt that the Liberal party, as a whole, was the party to voice their inmost convictions. They were not for party before everything else, like Mr. Balfour. Party was not worshipped as a fetish. No; they accepted party as a working medium, as all true Liberals did. There was little patience among them for that man who took airs and said, "I'm indepen- dent; I'm not touched by party." Wear, hear, and applause.) His position was admissible I academically, but shamefully foolish in practice. The fact was, they were young men, whose aspirations found them most in the contemplation of the stirring work of their forefathers in Liberalism. They had a !sense of the inherent greatness of man. That was the keynote to their character and conviction. Their I moral sensibility was the source of all their thought and I action, and they lived in days when it was only too plainly seen that humanity was undergoing degradation at the hands of the unscrupulous men. Their national life suffered and pined, owing to the lack of that moral I enthusiasm in their political life. Continuing Mr. 1 Gray gave a resunie of the work of the Association pointing out that a biographical committee had been formed with the object of getting up the biographies of our great statesmen of all shades of opinion, past and present, and would from time to time give them to the Association, while a political literature committee had in hand the arrangement of a sinAl yet choice library of political works which would be at the dis- posal of the members. Mr. Gray said they would see from what little they had already done that the association did exist for the moral and intel- lectual education of their young n4en in political subjects. They sought to drive home the fact that there was really a science of government. INTany people did not seem to have arrived at that point. They existed as an association to teach that truth. (Applause). All that was done with a view to rendering help in pro- gressive politics. To that was united mbral enthusiasm for they welcomed with open arms the passion of a man when wrong had been inflicted upon a portion of the people by an unjust piece of legislation. They abhorred the deal, listless, apathetic indifference, which accepted anything and everything without discriminating regard. It was that that beggared and killed the State. Dealing with political questions Mr. Gray said the ex- ploitation of the unemployed in the interest of the Pro- tectionist propaganda was a mean and sordid trick, occurring as it did in the midst of winter. A slight re- flection would show the hollowness of the appeal. The very character of the Chamberlain platform condemned it.lt would take a great deal to convince them that those men were consumed with any great desire that workers should be better paid. He hop-NI the working men of Warrington would see through the business and would help to silence that quackery. Mr. Gray touched upon the Chinese Labour question and continuin,, s said they had been accustomed during the last few years to see moral considerations over-ridden with the material. The lust for gold had destroyed all moral issues. rhe liquor traffic was a foe to healthy domestic legisla- tion and a grim menace to individual and commercial happiness. They also wanted to see every boy and girl, , whether he or she he poor, given such knowledge to fit them for the strenous life. Slumdoni stood as a mockery against civilization. There were a thousand and one internal reforms required, ere justice warranted the cry of "Empire." They confidently believed that every sound Liberal leader in Warrington was with that Association and was prepared to help them. Nothing would please them better than to think they acted as a tributary or auxiliary power to the life of the War- rington Liberal Association. In the coming election they were going to show fight. Their prospective can- didate bore a revered name and they were sufficiently optiMistic to believe that he would be returned by a 'triumphant majority. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN said the statement by Mr. Gray had well voiced the earnestness and enthusiasm which char- acterized the party in the old days and which was being evidenced at the present time. He (the chairman) was pleased to recognize the spirit in which the Assopiation was formed. He thought it was needed and that it would do a great deal to infuse into the Liberal patty that spirit which desired to elevate politics to a higher platform. (Hear, hear.) He would like at some future meeting of the Association to deal with the economic aspect of the fiscal question as the principal argument that would be brought forward, and which would have an influence upon many of those who did not think the subject out for themselves, was the idea that, Protection would bring more work to the

===== 0002601_19051216_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
country. That was the argument which it was the duty of both the senior and junior associations to knock thestulang out of—(applause)—because it was altogether lacking in truth and logic. It had been a mystery to him during the last three years, and he had again and again asked the question whether a man with a mind that had had a training like Mr. Chamber- lain's, really believed the nonsense be talked. (Laughter.) He (Mr. Parker) really thought he did not. (Laughter). He liked to think charitably of Mr. Chamberlain and his idea was that the ex-colonial secre- tary was so consumed with the one idea—wkich he Mr, Parker) believed to be sincere and to have become a ruling principle in his mind—that by this taxation or preference a mighty empire could be built un, that he was willing to sacrifice the economic part. When Mr. Chamberlain in his speech on Friday ventured briefly to touch upon the economic aspect he did not appear to be at all sure of his ground. He (Mr. Parker) was very glad indeed that the highest aspects of politics were not going to be lost sight of and that the part that would be played by the Junior Liberal Association would tend to elevate the lines on which the contest would be fought. Mr. Parker concluded by reading the following telegram from Ur. Crosfield : "Please convey ' to junior Liberals my very sincere apologies and great regret that I am, as you know, prevented joining them to-night. Crosfleld." Mr. Crosileld had hailed the formation of the association with pleasure and he hoped to meet the members on some future occasion. (Applause).

JOSEPIIIIIIIVTILORN moved :—" This meeting ex- presses its delight at the downfall of the Tory Govern- ment and the advent to power of the Liberal Party pre- sided over by Sir Henry .Canapbell-Baunerman. We earnestly trust that Mr. Crosfleld will be called by the party to champion the cause of Liberalism and Free Trade in this borough, and should this choice be made this meeting pledges itself to (1.) all in its power to secure his election to Parliament by a triumphant majority." He said the resolution was remarkable for the fact that it recorded the accession to power of the Liberal Government which was a matter on which they as Liberals had need to congratulate themselves. They must not, however, forget that at present the Government was in office without power. The momentum had yet to come from the constituencies. It was natural that they should ask at this juncture "What is Warrington going to do ?" Were the people going once again to support the party of reaction with a policy of despair in regard to the fiscal question, or were they going to join the forces of progressive Liberalism in favour of Free Trade? With an issue so momentous and a cause so sacred they could not afford to see it blighted by differences of opinion on local affairs. For over half a century the name of Crosfleld had been prominently identified with local Liberalism, and he hoped that in the coining fight Mr. A. H. Cros- field would be cheered by the rally of a united Liberal force and that tee result would exceed his most sanguine expectations. (Applause.) Mr. W. Ginter seconded. They were all, he said delighted that there was at last an end to ten years of misgovernment and what had been for the major part rual-administration. Our late rulers seemed to have been trying to build up a reputation for themselves by aiming at the aggrandisement of the empire. They had thought imperially ; they had spoken imperially, and . above all they had spent imnerially. (Laughter.) As junior Liberals they were glad to see Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman had come into power, for they believed no-one had done more for the Liberal party of late years than the new Prime Minister. They could not fail to admire the splendid mariner in which he had stuck to his guns and led the party through a period of darkness and the silent contempt with which he had treated his calumniators. Above all others he was the , man to bring about the great social reforms which were so urgently needed. (Applause.) The resolution was enthusiastically carried, CNlrteillOr JAMES EVANS addressed the gathering and said the coming fight would be one of the most impor- tant that had taken place in his time. It behoved every , Liberal and every level-headed citizen to buckle on the I armour and take a determined stand on the question of whether the country was to revert back or to progress.He did not believe any Liberal Government would take important steps such as the late government had taken without consulting the country. One of the planks of Liberalism had always been to listen to the voice of the people. (Applause.) Alderman PIERPOINT said be had been in his time a very active worker on behalf of Liberalism in the town. (Hear, hear.) He well remembered the first election which took place in the town in 1317 when the late Sir Gilbert Greenall was returned. As soon as he (Alderman Pierpoint) came under the influence of Cobden and Bright he became a Radical among Radicals and he was pleased to-day to be able to say that there was no more earnest Radical in Warrington than he was. (Applause.) If at any time he could do anything on behalf of the party in Warrington it would give him the greatest pleasure to do so. (Applause.) He fought to the best of his ability on behalf of the late Mr. John Crosfield and nothing could give him greater pleasure than to see that they were going to have a straight- forward light in Warrington andithat Mr. Arthur Crosfleld would be the Liberal candieate. With reference to his esteemed relative and friend the sitting member, as a friend and as a gentleman in every sense of the word Mr. Pierpoint had his best wishes. But as a politician, having imbibed as Mr. Pierpoint had to the fullest extent the pernicious doctrines of Joseph Chamberlain, he (the speaker) felt utterly ashamed of him. He contended that just as he was thoroughly ashamed of the conduct of Mr. Chamberlain so he was justified in being ashamed of any man who would do his utmost to assist Mr. Chamberlain in his nefarious designs. The question of Free Trade and Protection, to his mind, was not open for discussion. He hoped that every sensible man in Warrington, whether he was a Liberal or a moderate Conservative. could not but have one feeling with reference to Pro- tection. Could it be a good thing to make the food of the people dearer ? (" No.") Where was the benefit to come from ? Were the children to be limited in the necessary food to satisfy the greed of some people who believed in Protection ? He did not think for a moment that it was justified in the eyes of any man that Protection should be brought about. To his mind it was utterly beyond argument. At the same time he supposed the question would have to be argued to some extent on the public platform, with the result that Protection would be driven into that obscurity from which it ought never to have emerged. (Applause.) The Rev. J. L. MILNE asked his hearers to remember that theirs was a righteous cause. Let them think of the war in South Africa and of the increased poverty that abounded ; of the favour shown to the drink trade; of the abomination in South Africa to cram the pockets of those who were already rich. Let them fight with a perfect hope that the righteous cause triumped. (Applause). The Rev. C. FIARVE COOK said he was glad that in the offices of the new Government room bad been found for the first time in our history for a man of the Labour party. (Applause.) It placed a wonderful significance on the old saying: " Slow rises worth with poverty oppressed." He wanted the members of the Junior Liberal Association to understand that every polititian ought to feel as keenly and as deeply as the ordained man to the ministry ought to feel, that he was ordained to politics. Until politicians were distinctly convinced that they were ordained to politics as truly as any man was ordained to the ministry of the pulpit, that every man who took up politics was called to it and biad that "call" as distinctly as the man who was called to the ministry of the pulpit, until that be the spirit and temper that possessed especially the young politicians, we should not lift our politics to that serener atmosphere and higher plane to which the Presid- ent had referred. They must remember that ours was the golden age —toot these yeasting, fermenting, upheaving times were golden. To dream of a past or a future that Was golden was to desecrate the present if they did not realize that the present was the golden age. Every young politician should be a poet— not a rhyruster or a man that could dabble with verse and string adjectives and verbs together, but who had acquired great and lofty lyrics that spoke the great passion of humanity.He also asked the members to make history one of their special studies, for history was the science of man—the truth about man.

On the motion of Mr. A. TILLING seconded by Mr. E. OAIIORNE a vote of thanks was accorded to the chair- man and the meeting closed ‘rith the singing of the National Anthem.

FREE TRADE LEAGUE'S CAMPATGN. ADVICE TO CONSERVATIVES.

A public meeting arranged by the Warrington branch of the Free Trade League was hold in Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Thursday evening, Mr. James Kenworthy pre- siding over a fair attendance. The principal speaker was Mr. F. R. B. LiNDSFILL, late Conservative agent for the Altrincham Divi- sion, who showed that Free Trade lay at the I basis of our prosperity. Protection, he contended, would reduce work instead of giving more, and the consumer would have to pay an increased price for his goods. According to the Board I of Trade,he was unable to find that any of the industries which Mr. Chamberlain said were " going " were not in lan improved condition. The speaker complained of Mr 'Balfour's lack of straigthforwardness on the question and said that what was really before the country was Mr. Chamberlain's policy or nothing. It was the duty of Conservatives to put principle before party on a vital question like this and vote for the system under which we had attained a greater measure of wealth and prosperity and a greater amount of honour than had previously been permitted to any nation on the earth. (Applause). I Mr. L. GumESING moved- "That this meeting is of opinion that the principles of Free Trade have been an unqualified success in the past and that it is in the best interests of this nation that such principles should not be deviated from." He hoped the Conservatives in Warrington who put principle before party would record , their votes for the candidate who promised faithfully to support Free Trade. (Applause.) ' IVlr.Rrany ARMITAGE seconded the motion which was. carried unanimously, and votes of thanks terminated the proceedings. s

publicity. When Mr. Moss challenged his diagnosis sug- gested that Mr. Moss should call in an independent medical man. That Mr. Moss had refused to do. He had treated the letters as the pardonable drivellings of a parent who considered himself to be injured. The last two letters which he had received from Mr. Moss had been distinctly abusive and imvted to him dig= honourable motives. Mr. Moss was evidently a gentle- man who liked to express himself in words but declined to express himself in action. Mr. Woodward said it was to him a serious matter when he received a letter which reflected upon one of their officers and he was glad Co hear that Dr. Adams was perfectly satisfied that the case was one of small-pox. Mr. IVhitley : It seems to me that Mr. Moss has done himself the greater amount of injury as he has gone about the village telling people that the case was not one of small-pox and thereby giving greater publicity to the matter. The matter was allowed to drop. St. Peter's Church Army Mission. Success attended the annual tea and concert in con- nection with the above which was held on Thursday evening of last week. The tea was served by the lady friends of the mission. The concert was an attractive one, and consisted of selections by Master Tom Barton's band, and Mr. Hardley's English Concertina Quartet Party, which were well received. Solos were rendered by Miss Sumner, and Messrs. Taylor and C. L. Savage, duets by Messrs. Savage and Houghton, mandoline solo by MN. Barrett, a recitation by Mrs. &Wage, and a Lancashire sketch by Mr. Hands. The items were eicellently rendered and were enthusiastic- al!y received. The Rev. D. R. James, curate, presided in the absence of the Vicar, and congratulated the mission workers on the earnest work they were doing in the parish: Votes of thanks were accorded to the chairman and artistes, the doners of the tea, and the ladies who had so successfully carried out the arrangements.

I tried one medicine and another and dragged on till I grew very weak and nervous. I could not bear the thought of food and when I did eat, as I had to do, it all returned. At times I could not keep even a drop of milk on my stomach. I had awful pains too, in my head, chest, and back, and was bloated with wind. I grew so weak that when I went out I became sick and dizzy. I became depressed and nervous and would sit brooding over my helpless lot . . . For nearly a year I was un- der doctors, but it was not until I tried Mother Seigel's Syrup that any relief came. The first bottle did me a world of good, and when I had finished a second bottle I was almost well. I could eat and sleep, my pain and dizziness left me and I picked up so rapidly that soon I was completely cured. I take the Syrup now, when I feel the need of it, and recommend it to my friends." WARRINGTON AND THE DISTRICT LEAGUE. 2NO Divisio HALTON v. Sr. BaRNATIAS.—PIavei at Halton OD Saturday last. Lowe kicked off for Halton and St. Barnabas immediately began to press, and Hewitt forced a corner, from which M. Barrow opened the sore for the visitors. St. Barnabas, still kept up the attack and Hawthorn and Hewitt had hard lines in not scoring. Halton now asserted themselves and after some rushing tactics Lowe equalised. Half time one goal each. The second half, opened very fast St, Barnabas again forcing tho game, and from a well placed corner Allen put them one in front. After this play was very even. Halton played desperately hard to draw level, hut met with a sturdy defence. Final, St. Barnabas 2 goals Halton 1. In cOmmenting upon "The Liberal Ministry," the " Baptist " says : "The spirit and genius of Noncon- formity find, happily, full representation in the new Government. Churchmen et mint complain ; nor with Lord Ripon as Lord Privy Seal, can Roman Catholics feel aggrieved ;lint with our eve upon men like Mr. Asquith . Mr. Lloyd-George. Mr. John Burns, Mr. Tlirrell, Mr. Buxton, Sir Henry Fowler, Mr. John Morley and Mr. Bryce, we may be well content to leave the Education Acts to their early fate. Congre- gationalists and Baptists will deem themselves and their best convictions fortunate in finding several stalwarts of their faith chosen as immediate advisers of the Crown."
